L287: Patients with TA-TMA in the EAP received narsoplimab for an average of 2.5 months. The duration of treatment in the safety database appears adequate to support approval in the context of this rare, serious disease. TA-TMA is not a chronic process and narsoplimab is discontinued when a patient achieves all of the response criteria and no longer requires therapy for TA-TMA. It is noted that the pediatric patients had a longer exposure to narsoplimab and, therefore likely a higher number of doses which was by investigator discretion. The Applicant confirms that the longer pediatric treatment duration was due to investigator-directed continuation of therapy after TA-TMA resolution, influenced by relapse prevention considerations.
L288: In the EAP, the rate of serious adverse events (SAEs) was 46.2%. Serious adverse reactions included respiratory failure, multiple organ dysfunction syndrome, and polyserositis. Six (5.9%) deaths are listed as SAEs.
L289: The most common serious adverse reactions were in the system organ class of 'Infections and Infestations', which is again similar to what was observed the TMA-001 study. Therefore, if the product were to be approved, serious infections should be included in the 'Warnings and Precautions' section of the USPI. Serious infections should also be further monitored through a postmarketing requirement.
L290: See Table 12 and Table 13, in the Appendix for the total serious adverse events for the entire EAP group, and serious adverse events for the pediatric patients from the EAP, respectively.
L291: 7. Clinical Pharmacology
L292: Although no new clinical pharmacology data was included in the BLA resubmission, this section includes a summary of assessment on dose and dosing frequency in adult and pediatric patients based on the additional clinical experience from the EAP and the need for supplemental dose
L293: Dose and Dosing Frequency
L294: The proposed narsoplimab dose and dosing regimen is 4 mg/kg, given as a 30-minute IV infusion (up to 370 mg per infusion) once weekly. This was the dose and dosing regimen studied in Study OMS721-TMA-001. As noted in the Integrated Review associated with the original BLA submission (DARRTS date: 10/15/2021), the Applicant selected a dose of 4 mg/kg based on the pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) results from Study OMS721-NHV-002 in healthy patients. This dose was expected to maintain C4d inhibition (marker for lectin pathway inhibition) greater than 80% for about 168 hours providing the basis for administering once weekly.
L295: The efficacy and safety of narsoplimab 4 mg/kg administered once weekly has been demonstrated in OMS721-TMA-001. In the expanded access program (EAP), the Applicant evaluated a more frequent regimen of 4 mg/kg administered twice weekly due to the high-risk nature of patients typically enrolled under expanded access and further supported by the established safety profile from OMS721-TMA-001. Duration of dosing in OMS721-TMA-001 was 4 to 8 weeks. The duration and frequency of treatment administered to patients under the EAP varied and were determined by the treating physician based on the individual's clinical response. Median duration of dosing in the EAP was 5.7 weeks, and median number of doses was 9. The twice weekly regimen is supported by the efficacy and safety data published in EAP Subgroup 1 (Castelli et al), reviewed and confirmed by the Agency.
L296: As OMS721-TMA-001 studied a once weekly regimen and the EAP employed a twice weekly regimen of narsoplimab, an information request was sent to understand the Applicant's approach to dosing and administration in the product's prescribing information. The Applicant clarified that once weekly administration is appropriate as the starting regimen, however, given inter-patient variability in disease severity and level of lectin pathway hyperactivation, increasing the frequency to twice weekly administration may be clinically justified in patients who do not respond adequately to narsoplimab treatment. Direct evidence showing improvement in patient response escalating from once weekly to twice weekly regimen is not available. However, from a PD perspective, a more frequent (e.g., twice weekly) dosing regimen is expected to maintain higher lectin pathway inhibition throughout the inter-dosing interval compared to once weekly regimen. It is also reassuring that the safety profile of a twice weekly regimen employed in the EAP is acceptable ensuring no additional safety risk to patients escalating to a more frequent regimen. Although the typical duration of treatment for TA-TMA is relatively shorter (&lt;10 weeks), as patients are frequently monitored, it is still practically feasible to escalate the dosing frequency based on inadequate treatment response.
L297: Narsoplimab-wuug
L298: Therefore, based on the totality of data, narsoplimab can be initiated once weekly in patients with TA-TMA. The dosing frequency can be increased to twice weekly if there is no improvement in TA-TMA signs and symptoms.
L299: While the proposed dose is narsoplimab 4 mg/kg (not to exceed 370 mg), patients in Study TMA-001 following amendment #10 received a flat dose of 370 mg once weekly if they weighed \geq 50 kg. Only patients weighing &lt; 50 kg received a body weight adjusted dose of 4 mg/kg once weekly. Similarly, in the EAP, patients weighing \geq 50 kg received 370 mg twice weekly and patients weighing &lt; 50 kg received 4 mg/kg twice weekly. As seen from Figure 11 in the Integrated Review associated with the original BLA submission, a flat dose of 370 mg results in higher exposure compared to the body weight adjusted dose of 4 mg/kg. As the safety of 370 mg in patients weighing \geq 50 kg is demonstrated, the Agency recommends that the Applicant proposed dosing instructions i.e., 4 mg/kg (up to a maximum of 370 mg), be revised to recommend a flat dose of 370 mg in patients weighing \geq 50 kg and 4 mg/kg in patients weighing &lt; 50 kg.
L300: Dosing in pediatric patients
L301: No pediatric patients were enrolled in Study OMS721-TMA-001. Clinical experience in pediatric patients comes from the EAP. Eighty-six pediatric patients with TA-TMA were treated in the EAP and their age at the time of treatment is shown in Table 10 below.

L304: Source: Applicant's response to Information Requests, September 2025.
L305: Out of 97 pediatric patients (&lt; 17 years old) treated in EAP Subgroup 2, efficacy and safety data from 6 pediatric patients were included in EAP Subgroup 1. The efficacy and safety data in pediatric patients aligns with that observed in adults. The median age of the 6 pediatric patients is 11 years, ranging from 6 to 16 years. Narsoplimab was dosed at 4 mg/kg twice weekly, consistent with how adult patients with TA-TMA were dosed in the EAP. No PK or PD data is available from the pediatric patients treated in the EAP.
L306: Narsoplimab-wuug
L307: No PK or PD data was available from pediatric patients. In response to an information request, the Applicant simulated narsoplimab exposures in pediatric subjects using the adult population PK model. Of note, the adult population PK model including a body weight effect on clearance (exponent factor of 0.726) was reviewed by the Agency with the original BLA submission and found to be acceptable to characterize the observed adult PK data. The simulated PK data in pediatric subjects suggested comparable area under the curve (AUC) at steady state for subjects \geq 2 years of age with either once weekly or twice weekly regimen of 4 mg/kg. Based on the general understanding of monoclonal antibody pharmacokinetics in pediatric population, the clearance on a per-kilogram basis is usually higher in pediatric patients compared to adults. As per expectation, the resulting narsoplimab exposures when dosed at the same 'mg/kg' dose in pediatric patients are not higher than what was observed in adults. The adult population PK model did not include allometric scaling on other PK parameters (e.g., volume of distribution), therefore the predicted concentration, particularly Cmax, could be under predicted in young pediatric patients with low body weight and adds to the uncertainty in projecting exposures without any pediatric PK data in patients &lt; 2 years of age. A post-marketing commitment is issued to collect PK and PD data in pediatric patients, including in patients &lt; \frac{(b)}{(4)} years of age.
L308: In summary, from a safety perspective the dose of 4 mg/kg seems appropriate in pediatric patients \geq 2 years of age. With regard to efficacy, the appropriateness of the dose relies primarily on the efficacy evaluation from the EAP. As stated earlier, although the starting regimen is 4 mg/kg once weekly, patients including pediatric patients have the option to increase the frequency to twice weekly if there is no improvement in TA-TMA signs and symptoms.
L309: Need for supplemental narsoplimab dose following plasma exchange (PE)
L310: During the review of the original BLA submission, the clinical pharmacology review team evaluated (b) (4) a supplemental narsoplimab dose of 2 mg/kg to be administered up to 3 times weekly for patients who undergo plasma exchange (PE). Although 5 patients received supplemental doses of narsoplimab because of PE, the effect of PE on narsoplimab clearance (CL) and its exposure was not investigated in a dedicated clinical study or did the Applicant provide adequate justification for the selected supplemental dose of narsoplimab post-PE. Therefore, an information request was sent to the Applicant during the original BLA review to specifically assess the effect of PE as a time-varying, non-constant variable on CL via population PK analyses to accurately capture the transient nature of the intervention. In response, the Applicant conducted a comprehensive reanalysis by implementing a modified population PK model incorporating PE as a time-varying covariate across 36 documented PE events with a median duration of 1.45 hours. The Applicant's analysis estimated a 37% increase in narsoplimab clearance during PE events, representing a statistically significant improvement in model fit compared to the original model. The Applicant also showed that because of the transient nature of the intervention, the effect of PE on narsoplimab exposure was negligible and concluded that a supplemental dose following PE was not required. The Agency's review of the Applicant's analysis showed that the impact of PE on
L311: narsoplimab CL is on an average 47% but with a wide 95% CI ranging from 24% to 124%. To improve the precision on the CL estimate, the Agency included a comment in the Complete Response Letter (CRL) to conduct a dedicated clinical study to methodically assess the effect of PE on narsoplimab clearance by collecting rich PK samples pre- and post-PE.
L312: In the current BLA resubmission, no additional data to understand the effect of PE on narsoplimab CL/exposure was submitted. The EAP protocol also did not specify administration of supplemental doses following PE. Although any new data was not generated, the clinical pharmacology review team concludes that there may not be a need for a supplemental PE dose because (i) the duration of PE is short, (ii) despite limitations the transient nature of the intervention had negligible effect on narsoplimab exposures, (iii) supplemental doses were not administered in the EAP, and (iv) the current dosage and administration instructions in the label provides the option to increase the dosing frequency to twice weekly making the utility of a supplemental PE dose limited. Due to these considerations, a supplemental narsoplimab dose following PE is not required.
L313: 8. Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC)
L314: Key points from the CMC consult review are briefly summarized here, the detailed, finalized primary review memo is in Panorama, dated 10/30/2025, and the quality executive summary can be found in DARRTS, dated 11/26/2025.
L315: The CMC reviewer has concluded that the supported preparation and administration conditions are D5W saline administered with PVC contact materials (infusion bag and line) with a polyethersulfone (PES) in-line filter. Further, for pediatric patient with less than or equal to 10 kg (the total dose is 40 mg, around 2 years old) should be diluted to 0.8 mg/mL D5W by using polypropylene syringe and syringe pump since the dosing is small and maximal volume can be used as 50mL. The use of syringe pumps is also supported based on the stability data and dose preparation data (Table 6) in the IR response (SDN 103, received on October 1, 2025). For pediatric patients with more than or equal to 10 kg can be diluted between 0.8-8 mg/mL in D5W by regulator IV with PVC with in-line filter.
L316: The suitability of preparation and administration conditions for narsoplimab will be further addressed in PMCs (see Section 8 below).
L317: 9. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L318: Pediatric patients were included in the expanded access program (EAP) for narsoplimab. The indication statement will include pediatric patients down to 2 years old. The youngest patient in the EAP was 4 months of age. The efficacy and safety profile of the drug appear similar
L319: between adult and pediatric patients. The review team expects the weight-based dosing to result in similar exposures and should not be higher than exposure in adults. In addition, a reportable PMR will be issued to establish a registry to characterize the long-term safety of narsoplimab in at least 50 adult and pediatric patients with TA-TMA.
L320: 10. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L321: An advisory committee meeting was not convened to discuss this application. No issues were identified that would have benefitted from a public discussion with external experts.

L331: 12. Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
L332: Based on the available data, a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy is not necessary to ensure the benefits of narsoplimab in the high-risk TA-TMA population outweigh the risks.
L333: Narsoplimab is part of the complement inhibitor class but has a different mechanism of action than other approved complement inhibitors. Certain approved complement inhibitors have a REMS due to their direct involvement in the alternative complement pathway which protects against encapsulated organism infections. Narsoplimab inhibits mannose binding protein-associated serine protease 2 (MASP-2) which is an enzyme in the lectin pathway of the complement system. The other two pathways of the complement system, classical and alternative, are not affected; and these two pathways are fully functional.
L334: Inhibition of MASP-2 increases the risk of serious infections, but it is not clear how it increases the risk of infections from encapsulated bacteria. Infections with encapsulated organisms in patients with MASP-2 deficiency have not been a common finding (Garcia-Laorden et al., 2020). TA-TMA is an acute, potentially life-threatening complication that typically occurs early after transplant. It is expected that patients will be treated in an inpatient setting and narsoplimab is not intended as a chronic or long-term therapy. The standard of care for monitoring and managing serious infections for post-transplant patients would be similar to the management
L335: of patients who are post-transplant, in general. Post-transplant patients are managed closely by a multidisciplinary team with hematology/oncology specialists who are knowledgeable about the risk of infections and management. This patient population is immunocompromised and at high risk of infection due to the recent transplant. The standard of care for post-transplant patients includes close monitoring for infections. Treatment with narsoplimab may increase the risk of serious infections; however, we do not anticipate a need for additional monitoring or changes to the standard of care. Patients who are candidates for transplant typically receive education on the risks and are expected to be knowledgeable about the infection risk and management. We do not anticipate additional mitigation strategies would be necessary beyond labeling to ensure prescribers are monitoring and managing serious infections
L336: Based on our understanding of the disease state, duration of therapy, and anticipated prescribers and patient population, our assessment is that a REMS is not necessary to ensure the benefits of narsoplimab outweigh its risks.
L337: 13. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L338: Postmarketing requirement (PMR)
L339: Narsoplimab is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement. The long-term safety of patients who receive treatment with narsoplimab is unknown. The goal of a patient registry is to provide continued follow-up of patients to characterize the long-term safety of narsoplimab in patients with TA-TMA. An important risk associated with narsoplimab is serious infections. MASP-2 is an effector enzyme in the lectin complement pathway, which plays an important role in innate immunity. In addition, autoimmunity has rarely been associated with MASP-2 deficiency, and since narsoplimab inhibits MASP-2, there is a theoretical risk of autoimmunity. This registry would provide safety follow-up data and a summary of the major safety findings for this population, including all serious infections and autoimmune disorders.
L340: 4938-1 PMR:
L341: Establish a registry to characterize the long-term safety of narsoplimab-wuug in at least 50 adult and pediatric patients with transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (TA-TMA), with at least 1 year of follow-up.
L342: The final study report should include a summary of the major safety findings for all patients including patient level data on narsoplimab-wuug dosing, serious infections, serious treatment emergent adverse events and laboratory data (i.e., CBC with differential and renal function tests), autoimmune disorders, concomitant medications, vital status, and cause of death for any fatalities.
L343: Postmarketing commitments (PMCs)
L344: No PK or PD data is available in pediatric patients who have been exposed to narsoplimab in the submitted clinical studies or compassionate use program. However, based on the general understanding of monoclonal antibody pharmacokinetics in pediatric population, the clearance on a per-kilogram basis is usually higher in pediatric patients compared to adults. Therefore, narsoplimab, a monoclonal antibody, when dosed at the same mg/kg dose in pediatric patients, the resulting exposures are not expected to be higher than what was observed in adults, at least down to 2 years of age. Below 2 years of age, there may be greater uncertainty in projecting exposures without any pediatric PK data due to maturation of clearance pathways. Therefore, from a safety perspective, the dose of 4 mg/kg seems appropriate in pediatric patients \geq 2 years of age. However, evidence from a PK study in pediatric patients would further support pediatric dosing, particularly in patients &lt; 2 years of age and provide data to lower the indicated age to &lt; 2 years old, in the future.
L345: 4938-2 PMC:
L346: Provide safety, pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) data in pediatric patients &lt; 17 years of age treated with the recommended dosing regimen of narsoplimab for transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (TA-TMA).
L347: In-use compatibility studies were not conducted under worst-case conditions. Thus, there is residual risk that there are real-world conditions which may adversely impact product quality during routine preparation and administration in the clinic. To address this risk, we request the Applicant's commitment to conduct in-use compatibility studies under justified worst-case conditions (i.e., agitation, light exposure, duration of storage, administration volumes) and propose mitigations in the labeling, as necessary. The Applicant should investigate the root cause of visible and sub-visible particle formation. Identified visible and sub-visible particles should be adequately characterized to support conclusions that the preparation and administration conditions are appropriate and risks from sub-visible and visible particles are appropriately characterized. We also recommend that you evaluate additional preparation and administration conditions to determine compatibility with narsoplimab to allow for further flexibility in administration (i.e., product contact materials, narsoplimab dilutions, diluents, or filters). All incompatibilities should be thoroughly investigated to inform labeling.

L355: 4938-3 PMC:
L356: Postmarketing commitment: Conduct a worst-case in-use stability study to evaluate the suitability of preparation and administration conditions for narsoplimab under clinically relevant conditions. The study should assess product stability under stressed conditions including but not limited to extended light exposure as mechanical stress from mixing as well as worst-case dilutions and volumes. All incompatibilities should be investigated to inform the labeling. Propose revisions to the labeling as appropriate.
L357: Final Report Submission: 10/2026
L358: 4938-4 PMC:
L359: Perform a reproducibility evaluation for size exclusion chromatography (SEC), cation exchange chromatography (CEX), and capillary electrophoresis (CE-SDS) reduced and non-reduced with appropriate stressed stability samples to support Vetter as a quality control testing site for narsoplimab stability samples.
L360: Final Report Submission: 10/2026
L361: 4938-5 PMC:
L362: Establish an appropriately justified drug product stability specification (b) (4) based on levels observed in clinical and commercial batches.
L363: Final Report Submission: 03/2026
L364: 4938-6 PMC:
L365: Complete a pending real-word shipping study for drug product transportation from the drug product manufacturing site to the drug product packaging and labeling facility and perform a real-world shipping study for drug product transportation from the drug product packaging and labeling facility to distribution centers and/or storage warehouses. The studies will be performed using representative commercial drug product lots under commercial shipping conditions and will include a total of three shipments, and the evaluation will include assessments for shipping temperature, integrity of the vials and/or packaging/labeling as well as product quality attributes from samples collected prior to and after shipment.
L366: Final Report Submission: 12/2026

L391: 14.2 Summary of Re-adjudication by Patient
L392: Patient (b) (6)
L393: Original assessment:
L394: The patient had a non-evaluable platelet response as the patient never engrafted platelets. During the initial review, there was insufficient evidence that the patient is a responder based on only two parameters of the response endpoint definition. In addition, cyclosporin was stopped 10 days prior to starting narsoplimab, and the review team could not completely exclude the possibility that the findings for the two other response parameters, an improvement in LDH and in serum creatinine, were attributable to cessation of cyclosporin.
L395: Re-assessment:
L396: The reviewer acknowledges that failure to engraft platelets in the post-transplant setting is well described as a serious complication and that initial review and adjudication of the patient as a non-responder may have been overly stringent. If a patient has not engrafted platelets and developed TA-TMA, a platelet count response would likely not be demonstrated with TA-TMA directed therapy in setting of platelet engraftment. The patient demonstrated a clear response in terms of a large magnitude of reduction in LDH (LDH decreased from 712 U/L to &lt; 1.5 x upper limit of normal) and serum creatinine (creatinine decreased 1.68 mg/dL to 1.03mg/dL). While not part of the TMA response definition as defined in the protocol, the haptoglobin increase from undetectable to the normal range is evidence of a reduction of hemolysis, a key feature of TA-TMA.
L397: With respect to the observed improvement in LDH and renal function potentially being due to discontinuing the cyclosporin 10 days prior to narsoplimab, it is possible that cyclosporin discontinuation improved renal function, as renal toxicity is well described with this drug. However, given the patient met the criteria for high-risk TMA as defined in protocol, it is unlikely that a substantial improvement in hemolytic markers such as LDH and haptoglobin would be attributed solely to stopping the CNI. As stated by Schoettler et al, patients with high-risk features should receive TA-TMA directed therapies (Schoettler et al., 2023). Further, there is recent literature and retrospective studies suggesting that outcomes are not improved with CNI withdrawal in patients with TA-TMA (Acosta-Medina et al, 2025).
L398: Narsoplimab-wuug
L399: The reviewer concludes there is sufficient evidence that this patient is a responder to narsoplimab based on improvement in LDH and decrease in creatinine in setting of failure to engraft platelets.
L400: • Patient (b) (6)
L401: Original assessment:
L402: The patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria, and the baseline serum creatinine was trending down prior to narsoplimab administration; therefore, the effect of narsoplimab on the renal response could not be determined. The platelet response is non-evaluable as the patient had not engrafted platelets, and thus, the patient only met one response criteria, LDH.
L403: Re-assessment:
L404: This is another case in which the strict TMA response definition was problematic in determining response to narsoplimab, as the patient did not engraft platelets so this component could not be assessed.
L405: The patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria at the time of study entry; however, it is notable that the patient did meet renal inclusion criteria at the time of TA-TMA diagnosis. In addition, the decline in renal function starts early, often with proteinuria, and the change in eGFR may not appear until further into the progression of the TMA. The improvement in renal function was likely due to withdrawal of sirolimus and tacrolimus (nephrotoxic agents); however, normalization of renal function occurred following administration of narsoplimab therapy. Further evidence of response to TA-TMA is supported by reductions in hemolytic markers of LDH and haptoglobin.
L406: In considering the totality of the clinical picture, it appears that this patient had a response to narsoplimab based on improvement in renal function and reduction in LDH in setting of failure to engraft platelets.
L407: • Patient (b) (6)
L408: Original assessment:
L409: The patient had a non-evaluable platelet response due to failure to engraft platelets. The LDH response was transient, and tacrolimus was stopped the same day as narsoplimab initiated so we cannot conclude that the LDH response or renal response was due to narsoplimab.
L410: Re-assessment:
L411: Consistent with similar line of reasoning as above, it is not unusual for this medically complex population to have platelet engraftment failure and therefore the entire clinical picture should be considered.
L412: Narsoplimab-wuug
L413: During a Type A meeting with the Sponsor on February 9, 2022 (DARRTS 3/16/2022), the Applicant clarified that the patient did not discontinue tacrolimus as stated in the patient narrative. The Applicant stated this was an error in the patient narrative and confirmed with documentation in the case report form that tacrolimus continued to be administered. Therefore, the intervention of administration of narsoplimab in setting of declining renal function provides evidence that the renal response was likely due to narsoplimab.
L414: Further, while there were increases in LDH (&gt;1.5x ULN) during this time period, upon review of the record, these lab values appear to be single values and not sustained. This patient also had multiple infections during the course of transplant and while receiving narsoplimab. When looking at the overall trend of LDH from time of administration of narsoplimab, the trend was downward, and the LDH response criterion was met.
L415: It is the clinical reviewer's assessment based on the totality of data that the patient did respond to narsoplimab given the observed improvements in LDH and renal function in setting of failure to engraft platelets.
L416: • Patient (b) (6)
L417: Original assessment:
L418: The patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria. The patient met platelet and LDH response criteria, but there was no evidence of clinical improvement, and thus, the patient did not meet response criteria. The patient also had a diagnosis of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA) and a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT) 80 days prior to finally meeting inclusion criteria of a negative DAT, calling into question as to whether this patient truly had DAT negative TA-TMA.
L419: Re-assessment:
L420: The patient did not have an evaluable organ function component of TA-TMA to assess response. However, response components of LDH and platelet count were met. As supportive evidence, hemoglobin also increased by approximately 2g/dL. While hemoglobin increase in the absence of transfusions was not a component of the response criteria it is evidence of reduction in hemolysis.
L421: Despite the positive DAT, it does appear that the patient had a diagnosis of TA-TMA. Recent consensus diagnostic criteria for TA-TMA does not include a negative DAT, unlike some of the previously proposed diagnostic criteria such as Cho et al., 2010 and Li et al., 2019 (Schoettler et al., 2023).
L422: It is the reviewer's assessment that this patient responded to narsoplimab. While there was no evaluable "clinical" component, the patient clearly had a substantial reduction in hemolysis. In addition, the patient was receiving platelet transfusions at the time of TA-TMA diagnosis and achieved platelet transfusion independence following narsoplimab, which represents a clinical benefit.
L423: • Patient (b) (6)
L424: Original assessment:
L425: The patient's neurological response could not be clearly attributed to a treatment effect of narsoplimab. Additionally, we could not conclude that the 4-week transfusion free interval was due to narsoplimab due to confounders. The patient did not meet three response criteria.
L426: Re-assessment:
L427: The patient met LDH and platelet count response criteria. Regarding the neurologic response, it is this reviewer's opinion that this is very difficult to interpret given the supportive care measures that were implemented to treat posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES). However, the clinical reviewer does acknowledge that a 4-week transfusion free period did occur, and this was likely due to narsoplimab, as it occurred in the setting of decreased hemolysis after initiation of narsoplimab therapy. The other concern was this patient was not considered a responder by the Applicant until 103 days after narsoplimab treatment. However, the clinical reviewer acknowledges that underlying comorbidities likely contributed to inability to assess response prior to this timepoint.
L428: It is the clinical reviewer's assessment that the patient did have a response to narsoplimab.
L429: 14.3 Additional Survival Data
L430: 14.3.1 Propensity Score Comparison with pooled dataset
L431: This subsection presents the comparison of patients from the pooled dataset of TMA-001 and EAP Subgroup (Castelli) (N=47) to KSTCG confirmed TMA patients (N=121). The distributions of propensity scores are shown in the following Figure 6. The patterns are similar to those reported in Section 7.2.2. There was partial overlap between the groups, and areas of limited common support were evident. In addition, some propensity scores were concentrated at the extremes, suggesting a potential violation of the positivity assumption.

The x-axis represents the Propensity Score from 0.00 to 1.00, and the y-axis represents the Count from 0 to 15. The KSCTG Registry arm shows a high concentration of scores between 0.00 and 0.25, with a peak count of approximately 18 at a score of 0.15. L437: Figure 7: Covariate Balance Unadjusted and After IPTW Adjustment (Pooled dataset vs KSCTG Registry)
L438: The figure is a line plot titled "Covariate Balance" showing the Absolute Standardized Mean Differences (ASMD) for ten covariates. The y-axis lists the covariates: HSCTDGDY, GVHD, LDH, RELDONOR, AGE, Infection, RD, MISDONOR, ND, SEX_M, and PD. Five lines represent different adjustment methods: Unadjusted (red line with circles), IPTW (blue line with triangles), IPTW (1% truncation) (green line with squares), IPTW (5% truncation) (orange line with diamonds), and Stabilized IPTW (purple line with crosses). The Unadjusted method shows significantly higher ASMD values (up to ~0.85) compared to the other methods, which generally show lower ASMD values (mostly below 0.25), indicating better covariate balance after adjustment.

L484: 14.4 Summary of Key Issues with the Original Submission
L485: A summary of the key issues with the original submission is described in the Appendix
L486: 1. There were concerns regarding the conduct and efficacy analysis of TMA-001. Although TMA-001 had a protocol with inclusion and exclusion criteria and response criteria, the Applicant repeatedly issued waivers in order to enroll individual patients who did not meet the inclusion criteria and the lack of adherence to response criteria undermined the credibility of trial results. The trial enrolled 28 patients with HSCT-TMA. The review team discovered 11 patients who did not meet the entry criteria for whom waivers were granted. The Applicant concluded that the response rate based on the enrolled population was 17/28 (61%, 95% CI: 41, 79%). The review team independently reviewed each case in detail and concluded that there were 5 patients judged as responders by the Applicant who did not meet the response criteria. The FDA analysis of the response rate was 12/28 (43%) (95% CI 24% to 63%).2. The primary analysis was to determine whether the study TMA-001 has demonstrated at least a 15% response rate of the study drug. The statistical assumptions in the protocol listed response rate of 15% for a non-treated group. However, they did not provide adequate justification for this assumed 15% response rate when the BLA was submitted.3. Lack of confirmatory evidence. The Applicant submitted a mechanism of action (MOA) paper to provide further support regarding the mechanism of action of narsoplimab. There is sufficient literature to document the presence of MASP-2 and lectin components in TMA and HSCT-TMA. The Applicant also provided data based on in vitro and in vivo models of TMA demonstrating that narsoplimab mitigated complement deposition derived from the lectin/MASP2 pathway, reduced markers of endothelial cell injury and apoptosis, and delayed though did not prevent the onset of thromboses secondary to endothelial injury. Although there appears to be partial mitigation of C-activation and delay in thrombotic onset by narsoplimab in animal and in vitro models, the Applicant has not shown sufficient evidence that this is expected to translate into a measurable clinical benefit, either directly or indirectly.4. The clinical pharmacology review team's assessment found no association between narsoplimab-wuug exposure or trough concentrations or the extent of complement component 4d (C4d) inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug treatment in patients with HSCT-TMA. Almost all patients who did not respond to narsoplimab-wuug treatment had consistently comparable or higher exposure than patients who responded to treatment. Therefore, although related to the mechanism of action of the drug, the extent of C4d inhibition does not appear to be predictive of treatment response in HSCT-TMA patients.
L487: 14.5 Summary of Regulatory History
L488: The following are summaries of the key regulatory interactions with the Sponsor from after the CRL to the submission of the response to clinical hold on XX date.
L489: High level summaries from the meeting are described below, see finalized meeting minutes in DARRTS for further details.
L490: Type A Meeting, February 9, 2022: Discussion of deficiencies of CRL and path forward
L491: The Division acknowledged that they did not object to the proposed efficacy analysis during the IND stage of development, however, upon review of the application, significant efficacy review issues (including high proportion of patients not meeting inclusion/exclusion criteria, missing response data that introduced evaluation challenges, and significant issues with response interpretability), as described in the CRL were identified. These issues were determined to be too great, given the small number of patients evaluated.
L492: The Division expressed concern regarding the robustness of the results of Study TMA-001 as the success of the trial hinged on a few patients with varying responses dependent upon different adjudications. The Agency reiterated concerns regarding the success threshold of 15% and concerns with the literature review to serve as an external control. The Agency also reiterated that the application lacked confirmatory evidence and the PD and PK data provided were not adequate.
L493: Formal Dispute Resolution Request (FDRR) July 15, 2022.
L494: The Applicant submitted a formal dispute resolution request (FDRR) to the Office of New Drugs (OND) on July 15, 2022. The appeal concerned the October 15, 2021, CRL issued by the Office of Cardiology, Hematology, Endocrinology, and Nephrology (OCHEN). On November 3, 2022, Peter Stein, MD, the deciding authority, issued a Formal Dispute Resolution Appeal Denied letter. In the letter, several paths forward were outlined. One approach would be to conduct a new trial—either randomized controlled or a single-arm trial using agreed upon responder criteria and compared to a well-matched historical control group and/or a pre-specified threshold based upon an independent literature assessment. Alternatively, the Applicant could consider a resubmission to address the CR deficiencies including results comparing response from Study TMA-001 to a threshold derived from an independent literature analysis and/or evidence of increased survival among patients from Study TMA-001 compared to an appropriate historical control group.
L495: Post FDRR Meetings
L496: - A Type B meeting was held on May 11, 2023 to discuss the Applicant's proposal for a resubmission which would include results comparing the response from Study OMS721-TMA-001 to a threshold derived from an independent literature analysis, as well as evidence of increased survival among patients from Study TMA-001 compared to an appropriate historical control group. In particular, the Applicant proposed to use data from
L497: Narsoplimab-wuug
L498: an independently conducted retrospective cohort study from the Kyoto Stem Cell Transplantation Group (KSCTG) for determination of survival estimates following TA-TMA diagnosis against which survival in OMS721-TMA-001 can be compared. The Division expressed concerns with this approach as highlighted in the Dispute Appeal Denial Letter, which noted survival results in the series reported by Matsui et al. (H Matsui, Y Arai et al. Blood Advances 4:2169-3179, 2020) with a &lt;10% long-term survival in patients with TA-TMA appeared to be an outlier compared to other literature reports.
L499: - Agency issued Written Responses on January 4, 2024, to provide feedback on the Applicant's resubmission proposal to provide evidence of efficacy based on the KSCTG registry to compared to survival data to Study TMA-001. In addition, the Applicant proposed to submit details from the single patient IND expanded access program and additional mechanistic information. The Agency expressed concerns with the Applicant's proposal in particular with comparability between the KSCTG registry patients and patients in Study TMA-001.- A Type B face-to-face meeting was held with the Applicant on September 5, 2024 to discuss Omeros' proposal for resubmission of their BLA using the KSCTG registry as an external control group and using survival data from Omeros' EAP for mechanistic data as confirmatory evidence. The Division provided advice regarding the Applicant's proposal, stating that they were concerned the Applicants proposal for a resubmission may not be adequate to address the deficiencies outlined in the CRL without individual patient data and the limitations of using the real-world data for supporting the drug's efficacy.- An Applicant-requested meeting took place on April 30, 2025. The Applicant received the acknowledgment letter for the resubmission of BLA 761152, which the Agency did not consider a complete response to the October 15, 2021 action letter, on April 28, 2025, and requested to meet with FDA to clarify the contents of the resubmission. The Applicant detailed which data was available for the KSCTG registry. The Applicant provided slides which stated: "We appreciate FDA's request for raw patient-level data, algorithms, and program codes that would permit the Agency to confirm which patients from the KSCTG registry (N = 2425) met diagnostic criteria for HSCT-TMA. Data necessary to verify or exclude a TA-TMA diagnosis are not collected or entered into the KSCTG registry. Omeros does not view the overall registry as germane to the external control in the BLA resubmission. The patients in the KSCTG registry who were diagnosed by their treating physician as having TA-TMA (N = 134) are the relevant population from which to begin the analysis of the external control." The Applicant also stated that "Additional data for the 134 patients, responding to nearly all of FDA's queries, have just been obtained and can be submitted shortly." The Agency stated that they would await the additional data and review once they were submitted.

L560: I. Executive Summary
L561: 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L562: On November 17, 2020, Omeros Inc. submitted an original new drug application (NDA) for narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of patients with hematopoietic stem cell transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (HSCT-TMA) based on data from an open-label, single arm phase 1/2 trial. Narsoplimab-wuug is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease-2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement. The proposed dosage of narsoplimab-wuug is 4 mg/kg administered as an intravenous infusion once a week (b) (4).
L563: HSCT-TMA
L564: HSCT-TMA occurs when endothelial injury, in the context of an allogeneic hematopoietic cell transplantation, causes microangiopathic anemia and platelet consumption, leading to microvascular thrombosis and fibrin deposition. HSCT-TMA may present with new onset or increasing proteinuria, hypertension, elevated serum creatinine and an increase in transfusion requirements with evidence of intravascular hemolysis. There is no consensus on a standardized diagnostic criterion, but most definitions include a combination of clinical and laboratory findings. The fatality rate of HSCT-TMA has been reported to be from 60-90% (George et al. 2004). HSCT-TMA is associated with increased morbidity from chronic kidney disease or other end organ dysfunction (i.e., pulmonary, gastrointestinal, neurological systems). The renal system is most frequently involved with acute kidney injury, chronic kidney disease, proteinuria, and hypertension. (Laskin et al. 2011). The use of high dose chemotherapy, radiation, calcineurin inhibitor (CNI) exposure, graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) and infections have all been reported as causative factors.
L565: Current Treatment Options
L566: There are no FDA approved products for the treatment of HSCT-TMA. The first line treatment of HSCT-TMA is generally limited to supportive therapy. Initial first line management has included withdrawal or minimization of CNIs (i.e., sirolimus, tacrolimus or both) as these agents have been implicated as risk factors although there is ongoing debate as to whether withdrawal is beneficial. Changing the immunosuppressive regimen is another important initial management step in patients with HSCT-TMA, but may take 1-2 weeks before an effect is observed. Additional measures include treatment of infections, GVHD and aggressive hypertension management.
L567: Off-label medications such as rituximab*1, defibrotide*2 and complement inhibitors such as C5 inhibitors (ravulizumab3, eculizumab4)* have emerged as potential therapeutic agents in HSCT-TMA. There is no accepted pharmacodynamic marker to diagnose HSCT-TMA, or to assess prognosis/follow response to treatment, although several are being evaluated and used in clinical studies.
L568: Study OMS-721-TMA-001 (Study TMA-001)
L569: The Applicant conducted Study OMS721-TMA-001 (from here on referred to as Study TMA-001) in patients with HSCT-TMA. Study TMA-001 was originally designed as a 3-stage, open-label single-arm, ascending-dose-escalation study of narsoplimab-wuug evaluating patients with atypical hemolytic anemia (aHUS), TTP or HSCT-TMA. Stage 2 was a cohort expansion stage and Stage 3 was an optional extension of dosing for patients who completed stage 2. Enrollment of patients with TTP ended early as a treatment effect over standard of care was not observed, and enrollment of patients with aHUS was stopped early when a dedicated Phase 3 study was initiated for patients with aHUS.
L570: Stage 2 of Study TMA-001 enrolled 28 patients with HSCT-TMA. Stage 2 of the trial consisted of a 4-week dosing period with optional extension of 4 additional weeks of dosing in Stage 3. Eligible patients were enrolled if they had a diagnosis of HSCT-TMA that persisted at least 2 weeks following modification or discontinuation of CNI treatment or at least 30 days after transplant with
L571: 1 Rituximab is CD20-directed cytolytic antibody indicated for the treatment of adult patients with Non-Hodgkin's Lymphoma, adult patients with chronic lymphocytic leukemia, rheumatoid arthritis in combination with methotrexate in adult patients, granulomatosis with polyangiitis and microscopic polyangiitis in adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older, moderate to severe pemphigus vulgaris in adult patients.
L572: 2 Defibrotide is indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients with hepatic veno-occlusive disease (VOD). The mechanism of action has not been fully elucidated.
L573: 3 Ravulizumab is a complement inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients one month of age and older with paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH) and atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS).
L574: 4 Eculizumab is a complement inhibitor indicated for the treatment of patients with PNH, treatment of patients with aHUS, treatment of generalized myasthenia gravis in adult patients, treatment of neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder in adult patients.
L575: BLA 761152
L576: Narsoplimab-wuug
L577: platelet count &lt;150,000/uL, evidence of microangiopathic hemolysis (presence of schistocytes, serum LDH &gt; upper limit of normal (ULN), or haptoglobin &lt; lower limit of normal), and renal dysfunction.
L578: There was no internal control group.
L579: Statistical Methods
L580: The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of HSCT-TMA patients who responded to narsoplimab-wuug treatment. Treatment response was defined as both an improvement in TMA- associated laboratory markers (Requirement 1): platelet count and LDH, as well as improvement in clinical status (Requirement 2): either improvement in organ function (renal, pulmonary, GI or neurological) at any time point post-baseline during the study, or freedom of transfusion defined as no transfusions (RBC, platelet) for at least 4 weeks from the last transfusion except for patients who died within 4 weeks of the last transfusion. Improvement in organ function was determined by a clinical review of data by the local site investigators.
L581: The primary analysis was to determine whether the study TMA-001 has demonstrated at least a 15% response rate of the study drug. The statistical assumptions in the protocol listed response rate of 15% for a non-treated group. However, they did not provide adequate justification for this assumed 15% response rate when the BLA was submitted. As noted above, there was no control group in the study; therefore, upon request, the Applicant submitted a literature-based review to justify the selected response rate from an external control group (see below).
L582: Trial Conduct and Analyses of Efficacy
L583: Although TMA-001 had a protocol with inclusion and exclusion criteria and response criteria, the Applicant repeatedly issued waivers in order to enroll individual patients who did not meet the inclusion criteria and the lack of adherence to response criteria undermined the credibility of trial results. The trial enrolled 28 patients with HSCT-TMA. The review team discovered 11 patients who did not meet the entry criteria for whom waivers were granted. The Applicant concluded that the response rate based on the enrolled population was 17/28 (61%, 95% CI 41, 79%). The review team independently reviewed each case in detail and concluded that there were 5 patients judged as responders by the Applicant who did not meet the response criteria. The FDA analysis of the response rate was 12/28 (43%) (95% CI 24% to 63%).
L584: Literature-Based Historical Data of Untreated HSCT-TMA
L585: The Applicant conducted a literature review based on publications from 2000 to 2020 to provide relevant clinical data on HSCT-TMA patients with the attempt to include similar risk factors and inclusion/exclusion criteria to those in pivotal Study TMA-001 to estimate
L586: the response rate in patients not treated with narsoplimab, i.e., to provide justification for the 15% efficacy threshold for Study TMA-001. The clinical and statistical review teams identified a number of limitations to the use of the proposed literature based historical data to establish the response threshold. The limitations are outlined in the review. One hundred and seventy patients were identified for the final analysis for a historical control response rate of 32% (95% CI 26%, 40%). Although limitations exist in this exercise, at a minimum we can state that the chosen 15% appears too low and not supported by the literature review.
L587: Mechanistic Evidence
L588: The Applicant submitted a mechanism of action (MOA) paper to provide further support regarding the mechanism of action of narsoplimab. There is sufficient literature to document the presence of MASP-2 and lectin components in TMA and HSCT-TMA. The Applicant also provided data based on in vitro and in vivo models of TMA demonstrating that narsoplimab mitigated complement deposition derived from the lectin/MASP2 pathway, reduced markers of endothelial cell injury and apoptosis, and delayed though did not prevent the onset of thromboses secondary to endothelial injury. Although there appears to be partial mitigation of C-activation and delay in thrombotic onset by narsoplimab in animal and in vitro models, the Applicant has not shown sufficient evidence that this is expected to translate into a measurable clinical benefit, either directly or indirectly. It is possible that narsoplimab-wuug's inhibition of the lectin pathway is not sufficient to alter the outcome in HSCT-TMA in a sufficient magnitude to demonstrate clinical benefit.
L589: Exposure-Response
L590: The clinical pharmacology review team's assessment found no association between narsoplimab-wuug exposure or trough concentrations or the extent of complement component 4d (C4d) inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug treatment in patients with HSCT-TMA. Almost all patients who did not respond to narsoplimab-wuug treatment had consistently comparable or higher exposure than patients who responded to treatment. Therefore, although related to the mechanism of action of the drug, the extent of C4d inhibition does not appear to be predictive of treatment response in HSCT-TMA patients.
L591: Conclusions on Effectiveness
L592: The FDA review team concludes that the data presented in BLA 761152 fail to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness for narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of HSCT-TMA. This conclusion is primarily based on the lack of at least one "adequate and well-controlled" trial. In addition, there is insufficient mechanistic evidence that narsoplimab-wuug will have the purported effect. It is also notable that, although we have limited confidence in the estimated response rate of 32% from the literature review, this percentage falls within the 95% confidence interval for the FDA's calculated response rate of 43% (95% CI 24% to 63%) not supporting a treatment effect.
L593: To establish a drug’s effectiveness, it is essential to distinguish the effect of the drug “from other influences, such as spontaneous change in the course of the disease, placebo effect, or biased observation” (21 CFR 314.126(a)). This is the basis for the statutory requirement that approval be based on adequate and well-controlled investigations, as well as the basis for FDA’s regulations describing the characteristics of such investigations (i.e., design elements that are generally intended to minimize bias and permit a valid comparison with a control to provide a quantitative assessment of drug effect).
L594: There are certain circumstances when one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation with compelling results, supported by additional data from the natural history of the disease could support an application. However, Study TMA-001 is a single arm uncontrolled study and the lack of a concurrent control prevents differentiation of the treatment effect from the confounding therapies, competing risks, and durability of response in patients with HSCT related TMA. The Applicant has not provided sufficient evidence for a reliable responder rate in an untreated group. The Applicant has not provided substantial evidence of effectiveness to support approval.
L595: Safety
L596: The safety database is limited given rarity of disease and small sample size. Observed serious adverse events included infections occurring in 36% of patients but given the lack of a control arm, the level of certainty for causal association between drug exposure and risk difficult to determine. If a clinical benefit of the treatment can be established for narsoplimab for the proposed indication, the risks, e.g., infections, can be adequately addressed in the product labeling.
L597: Path Forward
L598: Additional data will be needed to demonstrate efficacy of narsoplimab in HSCT-TMA. Possible options include:
L599: If a reliable responder rate in a comparator group could be worked out, that might allow for retaining the current approach (threshold analysis). The Applicant could explore alternative external data (e.g. registries) to establish a responder rate. The Applicant would need to increase the total number of evaluable patients in TMA-001 so that the analysis isn’t entirely post hoc, for example, to 50 as we stated in the minutes to the January 29, 2019 meeting (see last paragraph of response of FDA to Question 2).
L600: Alternatively, the Applicant could conduct at least one randomized control trial to demonstrate the effectiveness of narsoplimab-wuug for the treatment of HSCT-TMA.
L601: The Applicant will also need to provide confirmatory evidence; what this evidence will consist of can be discussed in a post action meeting. Of note, if the current trial that Omeros is now running in HUS were to be successful, that data might be used as confirmatory evidence for HSCT TMA.
L602: 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L603: 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L604: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L605: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Analysis of Condition

HSCT-TMA is a challenging complication of HSCT associated with significant morbidity and mortality. The complex pathophysiology and multiple contributing factors converges on final pathway involving endothelial cell injury and complement activation. Risk factors leading to endothelial damage include calcineurin inhibitors (CNIs), chemotherapy, graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), total body irradiation and complement abnormalities. The gold standard for diagnosis is based on characteristic histological tissue findings however, bleeding risk often limits the ability to obtain tissue diagnosis. The lack of a consensus definition for diagnostic criteria and limited therapeutic options have contributed to challenges in describing incidence, severity, and defining a response. There are consistent reports that HSCT-TMA negatively impacts nonrelapse mortality and overall survival of hematologic malignancies. The clinical presentation ranges from mild, self-limited disease to multi-organ failure and death with involvement of the kidneys, gastrointestinal tract, central nervous system, heart, lung and serosal surfaces. Risk factors for HSCT-TMA include factors associated with HSCT, such as human leukocyte antigen (HLA)-mismatch, infections, GVHD, calcineurin inhibitors, high dose chemotherapy and radiation.


HSCT-TMA is a rare, serious life-threatening complication of HSCT in which endothelial injury and complement over activation results in microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and microvascular thrombosis. Patients are at high risk for significant morbidity and mortality.
L606: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Current Treatment Options

There are no FDA approved therapies for the treatment of HSCT-TMA. Management can be difficult and includes supportive therapies to include removing the insulting agent, transfusion support, and treating concurrent conditions such as GVHD and infections. While often considered as an initial step in management, modification and/or discontinuation of CNIs and/or sirolimus may not be effective as there is no clear evidence that discontinuation of these agents provide a benefit to patients with HSCT-TMA. The role of total plasma exchange in HSCT-TMA remains unclear and is often not beneficial. Off-label therapies such as defibrotide and rituximab have demonstrated variable response rates and have an unclear impact on mortality. Other complement inhibitors such as C5 inhibitors are currently being investigated and frequently used off-label in treatment of HSCT-TMA.

There is an unmet clinical need for an effective approved therapy for patients with HSCT-TMA.

Benefit

In a single-arm, open-label Study TMA-001, 28 patients with HSCT-TMA received narsoplimab-wuug every week for 4 to 8 weeks.
In the FDA analysis 12/28 (43%) (95% CI 24% to 63%) responded to narsoplimab-wuug therapy defined by an improvement in platelets, reduction lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) and clinical improvement. The sponsor had intended to demonstrate at least a 15% response rate (based on expert opinion of the probable response rate in an untreated group). A literature review intended to support the selection of 15% was not adequate from a methodologic standpoint, however. It is also notable that, although we have limited confidence in the estimated response rate of 32% from the literature review, this percentage falls within the 95% confidence interval for the FDA's calculated response rate and thus, study data do not support a treatment effect.

The clinical benefit of narsoplimab-wuug cannot be determined from Study TMA-001.
L607: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Risk and Risk Management

Primary safety results included 28 patients with HSCT-TMA from Study TMA-001.
The most common adverse reactions (≥15%) were infections, hemorrhage, hypotension, diarrhea, vomiting, nausea, abdominal pain, neutropenia, anemia, pyrexia, fatigue, hypokalemia, and back pain
Serious adverse reactions occurred in 61% of patients receiving narsoplimab. Serious adverse reactions in &gt;5% of patients who received narsoplimab-wuug included, acute kidney injury, confusional state, acute respiratory failure, neutropenic sepsis, septic shock, pulmonary edema and vomiting.
Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 7% of patients, including neutropenic sepsis, and septic shock.
Dosage interruptions due to an adverse reaction occurred in 7% of patients who received narsoplimab-wuug included Escherichia sepsis, pyrexia, pulmonary alveolar hemorrhage, and acute myocardial infarction.
Serious infections occurred in 10/28 (36%) of patients. Neutropenic sepsis and septic shock occurred in two patients with HSCT-TMA. The following was reported in one patient; bacteremia, Escherichia sepsis, fungal infection, gastroenteritis, infection, viral pneumonia, viral respiratory tract infection, urosepsis, and pneumonia.
One patient with aHUS in clinical trials had a treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) of granulomatous orchitis.


The safety database is limited, as would be expected given the size of the affected population.
The risks of narsoplimab-wuug could be sufficiently addressed through the Warnings and Precautions in the United States Prescribing Information (USPI).
To minimize risk, labeling should include Warnings and Precautions for serious infections and the risk of tuberculosis.
The risk of serious infections will be further assessed in a postmarketing requirement (PMR) should the product be approved, and can be negotiated in next review cycle.
L608: 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L609: HSCT-TMA is a rare disease that occurs post hematopoietic stem cell transplantation associated with high morbidity and mortality. There are no FDA available therapies and usual treatment is supportive care, modification of calcineurin inhibitors and off-label use of complement inhibitors. The nature of the benefit, size of treatment effect and clinical benefit of treatment effect cannot be determined based on the data submitted in this application. The safety database is limited given rarity of disease and small sample size. The therapeutic context in which this drug would be used is a condition for which there is high mortality with no available therapies. Observed serious adverse events included infections occurring in 36% of patients but given the lack of a control arm, the level of
L610: BLA 761152
L611: Narsoplimab-wuug
L612: certainty for causal association between drug exposure and risk difficult to determine. If a clinical benefit of the treatment can be established for narsoplimab for the proposed indication, the risks, e.g., infections, can be adequately addressed in the product labeling to support informed individual benefit-risk assessments by patients and providers However, at this time no conclusion regarding benefit-risk can be determined as efficacy cannot be established for this product.
L613: II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L614: 3. Introduction
L615: Product Introduction
L616: Narsoplimab-wuug (proprietary name, YARTEMLEA), a new molecular entity (NME), is a monoclonal antibody that binds to and blocks mannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease 2 (MASP-2), the effector enzyme of the lectin pathway of complement.
L617: The Applicant proposes the following indication: the treatment of patients with HSCT-TMA
L618: The proposed dosing regimen is to administer narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg once weekly, given as a 30-minute intravenous (IV) infusion (up to a maximum of 370 mg per infusion)
L619: (b) (4)
L620: (D) (4)
L621: Disease Background
L622: Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation-associated thrombotic microangiopathy (HSCT-TMA) is a serious multisystem complication of transplant that is associated with significant morbidity and mortality. Milder forms of HSCT-TMA can result in an increased risk of long-term complications such as chronic kidney disease, while whereas severe forms are associated with mortality rates of 60-90% or even higher % or more (Khosla et al. 2018; Epperla et al. 2020). HSCT-TMA is characterized by thrombocytopenia, Coombs-negative hemolysis (elevated lactate dehydrogenase, decreased haptoglobin, anemia), presences of schistocytes, and often renal injury or other organ involvement (Elsallabi et al. 2016). Extra-renal manifestations include central nervous system (convulsions, encephalopathy, drowsiness), cardiovascular complications (cardiomyopathy, myocarditis), and pulmonary and gastrointestinal involvement. It remains unclear if the high mortality rate is due to HSCT-TMA or a reflection of the severity of illness in patients with HSCT-TMA, unrelated to the etiology (Schoettler et al. 2020).
L623: The true incidence of HSCT-TMA following allogeneic transplant is not known as it is difficult to calculate given the lack of consensus on diagnostic criteria; studies have estimated the incidence anywhere from 0% to 64% (Khosla et al. 2018). One study cited an incidence of 8.2% in a comprehensive literature review published in 2004 based on 5423 allotransplant patients (George et al. 2004). A more recent review of nearly 2000 adult patients with HSCT identified 13% as having definite TMA (Postalcioğlu et al. 2018).
L624: Diagnosis is particular also challenging not only because of the complex and critically ill patient population, with but due to lack of a universally accepted diagnostic criteria. The diagnosis is difficult due to confounding complications such as graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), which can closely resemble certain aspects of HSCT-TMA (Khosla et al. 2018). Biopsy with clinical criteria can yield a definitive diagnosis, but many patients cannot undergo invasive procedures
L625: without a high risk of bleeding or other complications (Khosla et al. 2018). Histologic features in the kidney include thickened capillary walls, occluded vascular lumen and endothelial cell separation (Elsallabi et al. 2016). Common features of HSCT-TMA include the presences of
L626: schistocytes, elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), decreased haptoglobin, renal or neurologic dysfunction, thrombocytopenia and anemia.
L627: It is critical to differentiate HSCT-TMA from other TMA disorders such hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) and thrombotic TTP, in addition to disseminated intravascular coagulation and Coombs-positive hemolytic anemia. To aid in this, typically patients with a diagnosis HSCT-TMA are found to have a disintegrin and metalloproteinase with a thrombospondin type 1 motif, member 13 (ADAMTS13) activity level &gt;10%, normal coagulation studies and a negative direct antiglobulin test. Proteinuria and terminal complement activation were recently reported to be markers of poor prognosis in children and young adults with HSCT-TMA (Jodele et al. 2014). More recently it has been proposed to include elevated serum C5b-9 levels to the diagnostic criteria, in addition to hypertension and proteinuria as early clinical markers of HSCT-TMA (Khosla et al. 2018).
L628: The pathophysiology of HSCT-TMA is complex and continues to evolve as it is investigated. Fundamental to the pathophysiology of HSCT-TMA is endothelial dysfunction (Khosla et al. 2018). Inflammatory cytokines result in endothelial damage and the activation of platelets as well as other coagulation factors, this in turn leads to thrombosis and fibrin deposition in the microvasculature of organs with subsequent end organ damage (Kim et al. 2015). Complement dysregulation has more recently been recognized as an important component of HSCT-TMA pathophysiology (Kim et al. 2015). Neutrophil extracellular traps (NETs) have also been implicated as a biomarker in HSCT-TMA (Arai et al. 2013).
L629: Risk factors for HSCT-TMA include unrelated donor, human leukocyte antigen mismatch, high-dose chemotherapy, radiation therapy, immunosuppression agents (calcineurin inhibitors and newer mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) inhibitors such as sirolimus), advanced age, female sex, GVHD and infections (Kim et al. 2015; Elsallabi et al. 2016).
L630: The majority of cases present 20 to 100 days following transplant, although rare cases up to 2 years have been reported (Jodele et al. 2014)(Gavriilaki et al. 2017; Khosla et al. 2018).
L631: Current Therapy Options
L632: There are no FDA-approved therapies for HSCT-TMA. Response rates to supportive care and off-label therapies are variable and range from 25% to 93%, with lower response rates attributed to plasma therapy and higher response rates seen with complement therapies (Young et al. 2021). The first-line management of HSCT-TMA is to provide supportive care which includes minimizing the insult that is causing endothelial damage, this may be an effective treatment strategy in patients with mild-moderate forms of HSCT-TMA (Rosenthal 2016). This approach includes treating infections and/or GVHD and hypertension management (Jodele et al. 2015; Khosla et al. 2018). In addition, reduction or withdrawal of calcineurin inhibitors (CNIs) may be considered an initial step in HSCT-TMA management (Ho et al. 2005; Khosla et al. 2018). Sometimes an effective approach is to change the immunosuppression from a CNI to an
L633: alternative agent. Patients may not be responsive to withdrawal or modification of immunosuppressants and there is the potential to increase the risk of developing or worsening GVHD (Elsallabi et al. 2016; Li et al. 2019; Epperla et al. 2020).
L634: Therapeutic plasma exchange (TPE), an established treatment for TTP, is not generally considered to be as beneficial (Khosla et al. 2018) for patients with HSCT-TMA. TPE may be more effective when initiated early or when there is factor H autoantibodies (Khosla et al. 2018; Young et al. 2021). TPE is also associated with significant risks such as infection, hemorrhage, thrombosis, hypoxia, hypotension, serum sickness and anaphylaxis (Khosla et al. 2018).
L635: There are several unapproved therapies that are used for the treatment of HSCT-TMA with varying response rates.
L636: Response Rates to Unapproved Therapies
L637: Response rates to supportive care and off-label therapies are variable and are reported to range from 25% to 93%, with lower response rates attributed to plasma therapy and higher response rates seen with complement therapies (Young et al. 2021) with the caveat that responses described in literature are frequently based on retrospective studies, use different response definitions and do not control for biases.
L638: Rituximab, an anti-cluster of differentiation 20 (CD20) monoclonal antibody has also been used as monotherapy or in combination with other therapies such as TPE or defibrotide (Jodele et al. 2015). Jodele et al demonstrated a 67% response rate with rituximab with or without plasma exchange in 6 patients although this was single arm trial and prospective randomized trials needed. Rituximab is associated with adverse reactions including; infusion related reactions, peripheral edema and hypertension, as well as less common but serious adverse reactions such as hepatitis B virus reactivation and progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (Kim et al. 2015).
L639: Defibrotide, a polydeoxyribonucleotide salt, has also been used for HSCT-TMA, with response rates ranging from 61-76% (monotherapy or in combinations with other therapies), although prospective studies are still needed (Young et al. 2021).
L640: Other complement inhibitors are being investigated as therapeutic options. More recently, eculizumab, a C5 inhibitor, has been reported to be successful at resolving HSCT-TMA in 67-92% of patients in case reports and small studies to include pediatric population (Khosla et al. 2018). Many of the dosing regimens with C5 inhibitors in HSCT_TMA is guided by measuring CH50 and adjusting dosing schedule to maintain an adequately suppressed CH50 level. Larger, multi-institutional, controlled studies are need to better evaluate the use of C5 inhibitors in patients with HSCT-TMA. Reported case series have indicated a possible role for daclizumab and vincristine as well (Elsallabi et al. 2016).

L661: 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L662: Table 5. Summary of General Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
L663: CharacteristicDrug Information

Pharmacologic Activity



Established pharmacologic class (EPC)Complement Inhibitor

Mechanism of actionMannan-binding lectin-associated serine protease-2 (MASP-2) is a key enzyme for the activation of the lectin pathway of complement, which leads to cleavage of complement components C2 and C4, followed by C3 and C5 activation and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC). OMS721 selectively blocks MASP-2 and inhibits the lectin pathway of complement activation, which is postulated to reduce prothrombotic responses in thrombotic microangiopathy.

Active moietiesOMS721 is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody directed against MASP-2.

QT prolongationNot applicable since narsoplimab-wuug is a mAb

General Information



BioanalysisConcentrations of narsoplimab-wuug in human serum were determined using a validated ELISA method at the Bioanalytical Laboratory of (b) (4). The method included ELISA plates coated with MASP-2A, mouse antihuman IgG4 HRP conjugated antibodies, and TMB as the detection substrate. The method validation is acceptable per FDA's Bioanalytical Method Validation Guidance.

Healthy subjects versus patientsClearance (CL) in healthy subjects who received IV narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg once weekly for 6 weeks was 0.128 L/h and is similar to the CL of 0.122 L/h in HSCT-TMA patients who received IV narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg or 370 mg once weekly for up to 8 weeks.

Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen (or single dosage, if more relevant for the drug)In healthy subjects, PK parameters (mean (%CV)) following multiple dosing IV narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg are presented below:AUC: 2314 (27.3) mcg.h/mLCmax: 36.9 (18.2) mcg/mLCL: 0.128 (32) L/h

Range of effective dosage(s) or exposureNarsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg capped to 370 mg results in exposure that results in &gt;80% inhibition of C4d through the interdosing interval. However, it is not clear if C4d inhibition is reflective of clinical response.

L665: CharacteristicDrug Information



Maximally tolerated dosage or exposureA maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was not identified in any of the clinical studies. The Applicant selected 4 mg/kg for evaluation in HSCT-TMA patients in Study TMA-001 based upon mean trough C4d inhibition of approximately 80%.

Dosage proportionalityIn healthy subjects who received IV narsoplimab-wuug 2 mg/kg and 4 mg/kg once weekly for 6 weeks, there was a less than dose proportional increase in Cmax and AUC0-tau.

AccumulationMean (range): 1.31 (1.02 - 1.75)

Time to achieve steady-stateTime to steady-state: 15 days (after three once weekly IV dosing of 4 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug).

Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial formulationsThe proposed product is narsoplimab-wuug injection for infusion, 185 mg/mL, which is the same product and formulation used in the safety and efficacy Study TMA-001.
