L1055: Defibrotide
L1056: Two patients received defibrotide as a concomitant medication, both were nonresponders.
L1057: Lack of Adherence to Enrollment and Response Criteria
L1058: Not all patients considered to be responders by the Applicant met all three components of the primary composite endpoint. The definition of response was described previously (6.2.1.1 and 6.2.1.2):
L1059: In total, 3 patients (b) (6) who were considered responders by the Applicant never engrafted platelets and therefore were not able to be evaluated for a platelet response. Of note, one additional patient (b) (6) was reported as not engrafting platelets on the case report form, but after reviewing the narrative it was clear that this patient did have a platelet response, with a platelet increase from 17/\muL to 103/\muL. Therefore, this patient was considered a responder by the clinical reviewer.
L1060: The Applicant's rationale for including the patients with a nonevaluable platelet count was that the protocol states in section 13.2.5.1, "If a patient is not evaluable for a response criterion due to confounding factors, the nonevaluable response criterion will not be included in the determination of responder." However, we do not agree with this approach as it is unknown if data missingness would have affected the efficacy analysis. Patients with missing data should be classified as non-responders.
L1061: Two additional patients, (b) (6) should not be defined as responders. The Applicant's rationale for including the patient (b) (6) as a responder was a robust hemoglobin response (hemoglobin improved from 9.8 g/dL to 11.7 g/dL), in addition to a reduction in LDH and improvement platelet count. Although this patient did have a hemoglobin increase, this was
L1062: not a clinical endpoint of the primary composite endpoint and therefore this patient should not be considered a responder.
L1063: The rationale for the Applicant including (b) (6) is that patient had an improvement for neurological condition of posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES).
L1064: Narratives for these five patients are described below and in more detail in Section III.16.
L1065: Patient (b) (6)
L1066: The patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 21 post-HSCT. The patient entered the clinical study on day 35. At the time of study entry, the platelet count was 17 \times 10^9/L (normal 130-400 \times 10^9/L), LDH was 792 U/L (normal 125-220 U/L), creatinine was 0.73 mg/dL (normal 0.72-1.18 mg/dL), haptoglobin &lt;3 g/L (normal 14-268 g/L), and the patient was receiving transfusions. The patient started narsoplimab-wuug on day 38 and received 8 weekly doses. Per the Applicant, the patient met a response (based on TMA markers only) 7 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. This was based on a LDH reduction to 278 U/L. Platelet response was not evaluated as the patient had not engrafted platelets and remained transfusion dependent. The patient met a clinical improvement response of serum creatinine 37 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug with creatinine reduction to 0.57 mg/dL (baseline creatinine was 0.76 mg/dL). The patient did not meet a response based on transfusions. While not a component of the response definition, the haptoglobin did normalize on treatment. Of note, the patient had a HSCT-TMA relapse 62 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug, although this was complicated by neutropenic sepsis. The patient died shortly after. The full narrative can be found in the Section 16.
L1067: With a delayed modest improvement in creatinine, it is difficult to say that this is a robust response. In addition, haptoglobin was not part of the primary response criteria. In terms of a protocol defined response, this patient is likely a nonresponder.
L1068: Patient (b) (6)
L1069: This patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 23 post-HSCT. At the time of study entry, on day 31 post-HSCT, the platelet count was 10 \times 10^9/L (normal 150-400 \times 10^9/L), LDH was 712 U/L (normal 125-220 U/L), creatinine was 1.48 mg/dL (normal 0.64-1.04 mg/dL), haptoglobin was &lt;3 (normal 14-268 mg/dL) and the patient was receiving transfusions. The patient started narsoplimab-wuug on day 33 post-HSCT and received 8 once weekly doses. Per the Applicant the patient met a response (based on TMA markers only) 15 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. This was based on a LDH reduction to 225 U/L. The platelet count was not evaluable as the patient never engrafted platelets and remained transfusion dependent. The patient met a clinical improvement in response based on serum creatinine reduction to 1.2 mg/dL (baseline creatinine was 1.68 mg/dL) although this was not a 40% reduction in serum creatinine as specified in the protocol. The patient remained transfusion dependent. While not a component of the primary endpoint the patient's haptoglobin normalized on narsoplimab-wuug therapy. Of note the patient's HSCT-TMA relapsed 43 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug, but this was due to a transient increase in LDH that reduced back to &lt;1.5 \times ULN within one week.
L1070: The patient died approximately 200 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. A more detailed narrative can be found in the Section 16.
L1071: It is the reviewer's opinion that patient (b) (6) should be called a non- responder. There is an improvement in LDH and an improvement in serum creatinine could be considered a response although the LDH appeared to be down trending (see narrative in Section 16) prior to narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The patient was also receiving blood transfusions and did not have transfusion freedom, although this could be due to poor graft function. In the strict definition of a response criteria per the protocol, the less than 50% reduction in serum creatinine does not meet the response criteria definition for improvement in renal function. Although there was an improvement in LDH, the magnitude of response is not large. The improvement in haptoglobin is notable and once again, in a clinical setting the patient would likely be considered as having a favorable response to therapy, but in terms of strict adherence to the protocol definition, this patient likely a nonresponder or partial responder.
L1072: Patient (b) (6)
L1073: This patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 113 post-HSCT, based on colon biopsy. At the time of study entry, on day 121 post-HSCT, the platelet count was 22 \times 10^9/L (normal 130-400 \times 10^9/L), LDH was 328 U/L (normal 125-220U/L), creatinine was 1.9 mg/dL (normal 0.55-1.02 mg/dL), haptoglobin was normal at 221 (normal 35-273 mg/dL) and the patient was receiving transfusions. The patient started narsoplimab-wuug on day 121 post-HSCT and received 8 once weekly doses. Per the Applicant, the patient met a response (based on TMA markers only) 11 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. This was based on a LDH of 394 U/L (further decrease to &lt;250 U/L later in treatment course). The platelet count was not evaluable as the patient never engrafted platelets. The patient met a clinical improvement in response based on serum creatinine reduction to 1.06 mg/dL (baseline creatinine was 1.9 mg/dL). The patient remained transfusion dependent. Of note the patient's HSCT-TMA relapsed 15 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug, but this was due to a transient increase in LDH that reduced back to &lt;1.5 \times the upper limit of normal within one week. This occurred a few times. The patient died approximately 170 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. A more detailed narrative can be found in Section 16.
L1074: It is the reviewer's opinion that patient (b) (6) should not be called a responder. Given the complexity of this patient's condition which is described in the patient narrative in Section 16. The reduction in creatinine does appear to be &gt;40\% but the transient nature of the response to LDH is difficult to interpret and the patient did not meet all of the defined response criteria. The patient was also receiving blood transfusions (in addition to platelet transfusions) and there was no improvement with narsoplimab-wuug, although this could have been secondary to poor graft function. In the clinical setting, could consider this patient as having a partial response but in terms of the protocol defined response, would consider a nonresponder.
L1075: Patient (b) (6)
L1076: This patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 36 post-HSCT. The patient's enrollment into Study TMA-001 was delayed due to a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT). At the time of study entry on day 92 the platelet count was 14 \times 10^9/L (normal 142-350 \times 10^9/L), LDH was 550 U/L (normal 240-480 U/L), creatinine was 0.52 mg/dL (normal 0.62-1.06 mg/dL), and the patient was not receiving transfusions. The patient started narsoplimab-wuug on day 117 post-HSCT and received 8 once weekly doses. Per the Applicant, the patient met a response (based on TMA markers only) 228 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. This was based on a LDH reduction to 315 U/L. The platelet count improved to 196 \times 10^9/L (baseline platelet count was 65 \times 10^9/L). All the clinical components of the response definition was not evaluable. A more detailed narrative can be found in Section 16.
L1077: It is in the reviewer's opinion that patient (b) (6) should not be defined as a responder. The Applicant's rationale for including this patient as a responder was a robust hemoglobin response (hemoglobin improved from 9.8 g/dL to 11.7 g/dL), in addition to a reduction in LDH and improvement platelet count. In the reviewer's opinion while this patient did have a hemoglobin increase, this was not a clinical endpoint of the primary composite endpoint and therefore this patient should not be considered a responder. In addition, the response occurring at 227 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug is far beyond any expected response.
L1078: Patient (b) (6)
L1079: The patient was a 32-year-old male with a history of high-risk ALL who underwent a matched related donor transplant. Day 1 post-transplant, he developed mucositis requiring parental nutrition and opioid management. On day 6, he developed bacteremia (blood culture was positive for staphylococcal aureus), he was treated initially with meropenem. The patient achieved neutrophil and platelet engraftment on day 18 and 22, respectively. On day 88, he was diagnosed with oral GVHD. His cyclosporine was discontinued on day 145. On day 159, he had elevated liver function tests, liver biopsy demonstrated severe panlobular hepatitis with hepatocyte necrosis, concerning for aGVHD. The patient was treated with prednisone. He also had CMV reactivation. Around day 178, he was diagnosed with acute skin and liver GVHD, in addition to chronic GVHD of the eye.
L1080: On day 194 he was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA, based on thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH, elevated creatinine and anemia. The patient had been restarted on cyclosporin two weeks prior but this was discontinued once HSCT-TMA was diagnosed. At this time, he was also diagnosed with PRES. With blood pressure control the patient recovered. He also had a low-grade fever. He received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 201 and received 8 total doses. Response occurred 153 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug and was based on improvement in LDH, platelets, neurologic dysfunction and transfusions per the Applicant. Following initiation of narsoplimab-wuug he had recurrent episodes of delirium and confusion, this was thought to be secondary to steroids, and/or self-discontinuation of lorazepam and haloperidol.
L1081: It is the reviewer's opinion that it is difficult to determine if the patient's neurologic improvement was related to narsoplimab-wuug. While PRES can be a manifestation of HSCT-TMA, it appears the patient improved with blood pressure control, the timing of the improvement in PRES is unclear in the patient narrative and therefore the impact of narsoplimab-wuug is unclear. Euphoria, delirium and confusion occurred after narsoplimab-wuug was started so it is therefore confounded by a new treatment and was managed with multiple other therapies including; hyperglycemia control, steroid taper, lorazepam and haloperidol. It is the reviewer's opinion that this patient should be considered a non-responder.
L1082: In summary, it is in the reviewer's opinion that 5 patients (b) (6) (b) (6) did not meet all three components of the response definition and should not be called responders.
L1083: Significant number of patients did not meet the predefined inclusion/exclusion criteria but were still included in the study. Magnitude of serum creatinine change may be difficult to interpret for patients who did not have the inclusion criteria of doubling of serum creatinine from pre-HSCT level.
L1084: The Applicant states 11/28 (39%) of patients did not meet inclusion/exclusion criteria but were granted waivers. Waivers were mostly granted for the creatinine criteria not being met (n=6) and for a positive Coombs test (n=3). The Applicant's rationale for waivers included; a diagnosis of HSCT-TMA could be made based on other criteria, renal dysfunction is a late finding in TMA progression and patients with HSCT-TMA may have a positive Coomb's test due to transfusions. A list of all patients who received a waiver and rationale why the investigator chose to continue to enroll those patients in the study is listed in Table 113.
L1085: Exclusion Criteria of Positive Coombs Test
L1086: A positive Coombs test was an exclusion criterion in Study TMA-001. Despite this exclusion criteria 3 patients were included in Study TMA-001 who had a positive Coombs test. Patient's diagnosed with HSCT-TMA typically have a negative Coombs test in order to differentiate HSCT-TMA from Coombs positive autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA) (Khosla et al. 2018). AIHA occurs in 4-6% of patients following allogeneic stem cell transplantation (Barcellini et al. 2019). AIHA can have overlapping features of HSCT-TMA, such as hemolytic anemia, elevated LDH, reduced haptoglobin, increase in transfusion requirements, and/or thrombocytopenia when associated with Evan's syndrome. Therefore, to ensure an accurate diagnosis of HSCT-TMA, patients with a Coombs positive test should not be included in this study. Although, the Applicant reports that the while the local laboratory reported a weakly positive Coombs test, the central laboratory report was negative for all three patients. Therefore, in this reviewer's opinion it is acceptable to include these patients in the analysis.
L1087: Inclusion Criteria of Doubling Serum Creatinine
L1088: A key inclusion criterion for Study TMA-001 was doubling of serum creatinine from the pre-HSCT level. In total, 6 of the 28 patients did not meet the renal inclusion criteria of doubling of
L1089: serum creatinine, yet the Applicant claimed these patients had other laboratory markers or clinical findings to support a diagnosis of HSCT-TMA.
L1090: While not all patients with HSCT-TMA have renal dysfunction, the kidney is the most common organ affected (Khosla et al. 2018) and it is included in the diagnostic criteria by the Blood and Marrow Transplant Clinical Trials Network (Elsallabi et al. 2016). While serum creatinine is an imperfect marker for renal injury, this was a reasonable choice for inclusion criteria (doubling of serum creatinine from baseline) and was agreed upon by the Agency during the clinical development of Study TMA-001. Further, this provided an objective measurement for assessing clinical improvement. Therefore, ignoring this inclusion criterion is an important protocol violation. One exception is patient (b) (6) who had biopsy proven TMA.
L1091: Five of the 6 patients who did not meet renal inclusion criteria were still evaluated for the renal response endpoint. The one patient (b) (6) who was not evaluated for a renal response had a normal creatinine at baseline. Of the 5 patients who were evaluated for renal response, 3 were responders (b) (6) per the Applicant, however by the FDA only (b) (6) (b) (6) were considered responders by the FDA.
L1092: Patient (b) (6) was the only patient who was dependent on the renal response as the clinical component to meet the definition of response. While the patient had an elevated serum creatinine of 1.25 mg/dL at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis it had down trended to 0.78 mg/dL prior to narsoplimab-wuug administration. While the serum creatinine further decreased to 0.6 mg/dL on narsoplimab-wuug therapy, this change is not clinically meaningful. It is in the clinical reviewer's opinion that patient (b) (6) should not be considered a responder. Additionally, this patient had a nonevaluable platelet count (see discussion on patients not meeting response definition). The clinical benefit of narsoplimab-wuug in this patient is therefore not evident.
L1093: In addition, the Applicant reports improvement in renal function occurred in 18/27 (74%) of patients. It is in the clinical reviewer's opinion that renal function should only be evaluated in patients who had doubling of serum creatinine from the pre-HSCT level, to ensure a significant and meaningful magnitude of change. Considering only patients who had a doubling of serum creatinine, the proportion of patients who had a renal response was 15/22 (68.2%).
L1094: In conclusion, the number of patient waivers for inclusion criteria reflects the challenges in the diagnosis of HSCT-TMA and the lack of uniform diagnostic criteria as there can be a variety of factors in diagnosis that may lead investigator to conclude a patient has HSCT-TMA. The rationale for the waivers may not be unreasonable as change in renal function can be a late finding and DAT positivity may be impacted by transfusions; however, this aspect of the clinical course of TMA should have been considered at the protocol development stage.
L1095: Difficulty in Determining Magnitude of Response
L1096: The clinical significance of magnitude of change in LDH was difficult to assess in patients with LDH values just above the upper limit of normal.
L1097: Inclusion criteria included laboratory values consistent with microangiopathic hemolysis, including LDH above the upper limit of normal; this is consistent with most diagnostic criteria
L1098: for HSCT-TMA (Elsallabi et al. 2016). Most patients included in Study TMA-001 had a significantly elevated LDH value with a mean LDH of 580.5 U/L (range 237-1140 U/L), normal range can vary depending upon laboratory but usually range between 140 U/L to 280 U/L, consistent with hemolysis (although LDH can increase due a variety of factors i.e., tissue damage, liver injury, bone injuries, infections and more). There were 2 patients ( (b) (6) (b) (6) ) who were responders that met the inclusion criteria of LDH, but the LDH value was just above the upper limit of normal. The LDH values of these patients are described below. As the criteria for LDH response was based on an LDH &lt;1.5x ULN, it is difficult to determine the significance of LDH reduction as the patients described below already had an LDH &lt;1.5x ULN prior to narsoplimab-wuug treatment.
L1099: Patient (b) (6)
L1100: The patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 66, at that time the LDH was 221 U/L (normal range: 100-200 U/L). At the time of study entry on day 76 the LDH was 278 U/L (normal range: 125-220 U/L). Baseline LDH was 237 U/L (normal range: 125-220). LDH at time of first meeting response definition was 227 U/L (normal range: 125-220). Lowest LDH following narsoplimab-wuug treatment was 165 U/L (normal range: 125-220). See patient narrative in Section 16.
L1101: Clinical reviewer comment
L1102: Although the patient had a decrease of LDH per the protocol defined criteria, the magnitude of change is small.
L1103: Patient (b) (6)
L1104: The patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 76, LDH was not reported in the patient narrative. Screening LDH was 290 U/L (normal range: 125-220 U/L). Baseline LDH was 261 U/L (normal range: 125-220 U/L). LDH at time of first meeting response definition was 187 U/L (normal range: 125-220). Lowest LDH following narsoplimab-wuug treatment was 104 U/L (normal range: 125-220). See patient narrative in Section 16.
L1105: Clinical reviewer comment
L1106: While the patient had a LDH just above the ULN at baseline, the clinical reviewer still believes it is likely the patient had a diagnosis of HSCT-TMA. The patient had other features of HSCT-TMA including thrombocytopenia, anemia, increased serum creatinine, and schistocytes. While the interpretation of LDH response is limited with a baseline value just ULN, the patient had a reduction in serum creatinine and improvement in platelet count. To further support response the patient also had improvement in haptoglobin and hemoglobin.
L1107: In conclusion, while both patients ( (b) (6) ) had mildly an elevated LDH, diagnosis of HSCT-TMA and response could be confidently determined based on other criteria. The clinical reviewer agrees both patients are responders.
L1108: Neurologic Dysfunction
L1109: Improvement in neurological function was defined in the protocol as; (1) improvement in reversible neurological conditions (e.g., cessation of seizures); or 2) stabilization of irreversible neurological conditions (e.g., stability of neurological deficits following stroke without further deterioration or subsequent strokes). The neurological deficits considered for improvement were limited to stroke, seizures, PRES, and weakness. Improvement in neurologic function was evaluable in 6 out of 28 (21.4%) of patients (Table 24). Of those 6 patients in which neurologic function could be evaluated, 3 were responders, narratives describing neurologic improvement are included below.
L1110: In a Type A meeting on January 6, 2020, the Applicant asked the Agency if they agreed with the proposal to assess neurologic improvement. The Agency responded that they did not agree due to the difficulty assessing improvement in the intensive care setting. In addition, the Agency had the following comments: “The evaluation of weakness is difficult in patients with multiple comorbidities and who require intensive care treatment. There may be multiple causes for a patient’s weakness therefore we do not agree with the assessment of weakness to assess neurological improvement. In addition, please note that improvement in confusion, agitation, headache will be difficult to assess given multitude of contributing factors to these signs and symptoms.” Therefore, weakness while included as a neurologic deficit for evaluation, was not agreed upon by the Agency.
L1111: Neurologic improvement was difficult to assess in all three patients, this is described in more detail in the narratives below. Neurologic manifestations included PRES, confusion, delirium, and weakness, all of which can be multifactorial (i.e., concomitant medications, deconditioning, and concurrent illnesses) and were treated during with other therapies along with narsoplimab-wuug. For example, one patient (b) (6), had a diagnosis of Guillain Barré syndrome (GBS) at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis, therefore it is difficult to determine if the weakness experienced by the patient was due to GBS or HSCT-TMA. Overall, the treatment effect on neurologic manifestations of HSCT-TMA is difficult to interpret.
L1112: Narratives for Patients With Improvement in Neurologic Dysfunction
L1113: (b) (6)
L1114: Approximately two weeks prior to the diagnosis of HSCT-TMA the patient developed polyneuropathy with profound weakness restricting the patient to bed. The patient experienced improvement in polyneuropathy clinically and based on electrophysiological evaluation while receiving narsoplimab-wuug. The neurologic response occurred approximately 27 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug.
L1115: Neuropathy is not a common manifestation of HSCT-TMA. While temporally it appears that narsoplimab-wuug may have improved the patient’s polyneuropathy, one must also consider that neuropathy can be multifactorial. For example, the patient was on a cyclosporin taper at the time of improvement. In addition, weakness was not agreed upon by the Agency as a neurologic deficit to assess for TMA improvement.
L1116: (b) (6)
Approximately one day after the diagnosis of HSCT-TMA the patient developed PRES. Cyclosporin had been discontinued the day prior to the PRES diagnosis. The patient improved with blood pressure control. The patient developed euphoria, delirium, and agitation approximately 4 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug and was managed with steroid taper (previously had euphoria while receiving steroids) and supportive treatment. Approximately 25 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug the patient developed delirium and confusion in the setting of uncontrolled hyperglycemia and self-discontinuation of lorazepam and haloperidol. His neurologic symptoms improved.
L1117: It is difficult to determine if the patient’s neurologic improvement was related to narsoplimab-wuug. While PRES can be a manifestation of HSCT-TMA, it appears the patient improved with blood pressure control, the timing of the improvement in PRES is unclear in the patient narrative and therefore the impact of narsoplimab-wuug is unclear. Euphoria, delirium and confusion occurred after narsoplimab-wuug was started so it is therefore confounded by a new treatment and was managed with multiple other therapies including hyperglycemia control, steroid taper, lorazepam and haloperidol. It is the reviewer’s opinion that this patient should be considered a non-responder.
L1118: (b) (6)
The patient experienced intermittent seizures starting 5 days prior to narsoplimab-wuug therapy. The patient was treated with antiepileptics and narsoplimab-wuug.
L1119: Seizures can be a manifestation of HSCT-TMA. It is challenging to determine if seizure improvement was due to narsoplimab-wuug or treatment with antiepileptics. Of note, the patient was subsequently found to have post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder with a cerebellar lesion. This could have also contributed to the seizures.

L1124: GI Dysfunction
L1125: Improvement in GI function was measured by improvement in the gastrointestinal staging in the MAGIC criteria (6.2.1.2). One patient (b) (6) was evaluated for improvement in GI dysfunction. The patient narrative can be found in Section 16. The patient had a GI biopsy consistent with HSCT-TMA and GVHD. Melena improved approximately 12 days following narsoplimab-wuug administration. While temporally narsoplimab-wuug appears to have improved melena, this is confounded by the concurrent diagnosis of GI-GVHD which was actively being treated with steroids at the same time.
L1126: It is in the clinical reviewer's opinion that patient (b) (6) improvement in GI dysfunction (melena) was likely secondary to narsoplimab-wuug. Although the patient was receiving concurrent corticosteroids, it does not appear the dose of had changed and was relatively low (prednisone, 25 mg daily). Therefore, this unlikely impacted the response.
L1127: Time to Response and Duration of Response
L1128: Time to Response
L1129: Response was assessed anytime postbaseline therefore, there was a wide range of response times.
L1130: As stated in the Applicant's protocol for Study TMA-001, the primary endpoint which was based on HSCT-TMA laboratory markers could be assessed anytime post baseline. There is a wide range of response times from 7 to 228 days, with a median response of 36 days. There were two patients who met response criteria &gt;100 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug based on HSCT-TMA laboratory markers, patient (b) (6) at 228 days and patient (b) (6) at 153 days. Patient narratives can be found in Section 16. The remainder of patients had a response between days 7-77. It seems unlikely that these two outlier responses can confidently be attributed to narsoplimab-wuug. It is therefore, in the clinical reviewer's opinion that patients (b) (6) and (b) (6) should not be called responders. (Refer to Table 28 for brief narratives for these patients).
L1131: In the TMA-001 study protocol, time to response was defined as the time from the first dose of narsoplimab-wuug to the first date of platelet count and LDH response achieved. Of note, although per the protocol laboratory assessments were to be performed weekly, many patients who were hospitalized had more frequent laboratory tests. Time to response, based on these TMA markers was 36 days (range: 7-228 days). As this is not a complete definition of the primary endpoint the Applicant was asked to submit a time to response analysis based on all three components of the primary endpoint.
L1132: In the IR response from the Applicant, the Applicant presented time to response based on all three components of the primary endpoint. Time to response was calculated as the number of days between the date of the first dose of narsoplimab-wuug to the first date that the TMA marker improvement and clinical benefit was achieved. Based on the Applicant's analysis, the median time to response was 57 days (range: 13 to 228 days). This consideration will be important for providers caring for patients with HSCT-TMA. It remains difficult to determine
L1133: when providers should expect to see a response to narsoplimab-wuug given the wide range of response times.
L1134: Duration of Response
L1135: Duration of response was defined as the time between the first date a response is achieved and the first date the platelet count decreases by more than 50% from the maximum and LDH increases to &gt;150% of the upper limit of normal. The median duration of response was 561 days (range: 5 to 823+ days). In total 6 patients experienced a relapse based on the duration of response definition. As this definition of response is not a complete definition of the primary endpoint the Applicant was asked to submit duration of response analysis based on all three components of the primary endpoint.
L1136: In the IR response from the Applicant, the Applicant presented duration of response based on all components of the primary endpoint. The Applicant defined relapse as having a platelet count below a level equal to at least a 50% decrease from the maximum platelet count, an LDH level greater than 1.5 times the upper limit of normal, and a creatinine increase \geq 20\% from the minimum creatinine if the minimum creatinine is &lt; \text{ULN} or an increase \geq 40\% from the minimum creatinine if the minimum creatinine is \geq \text{ULN}. No patient experienced worsening organ function and transfusion worsening could not be defined for patients who achieved freedom of transfusion given its definition. Based on the revised analysis the Applicant stated the median duration of response could not be calculated. The range of duration of response was 13 to 794 days. Based on the revised analysis, 3 patients relapsed.
L1137: Summary
L1138: Taking into consideration the issues presented above and after review of each of the patient narratives, the clinical team identified five additional patients (Subject ID (b) (6) (b) (6) as not achieving response as defined in the protocol. The reasons for excluding these patients as responders is that these 5 subjects did not meet at least 3 components of the response criteria. In a clinical setting, some of these patients could be described as having a partial response but in terms of protocol defined response are considered nonresponders. Based upon this analysis, the proportion of patients in Study TMA-001 who responded to narsoplimab-wuug would be 12/28; 42.9% [CI: 25%, 63] of patients. A detailed summary of the patient's narratives can be found in Section 16; in addition, Table 25 provides a brief synopsis of the reviewers' concerns.

L1151: 6.3.2. External Literature-Based Validation of Non-Treated Response Rate.
L1152: Issue
L1153: The Applicant provided an external literature review to justify the response rate in untreated patients.
L1154: Background
L1155: The Applicant submitted an external literature review during the review cycle of the application. The submission of this additional data warranted and required additional time for analysis and thus a major amendment was issued to the Applicant on May 19, 2021.
L1156: The study design was a retrospective patient level data from published publications between 2000-2020 identified using PubMed electronic database search. The criteria for publication selection were adult patients and any component of the primary response endpoint used in Study TMA-001 trial and report of response and at least 1 risk factor present in the Study TMA-001 trial patients. The literature search identified 459 manuscripts of which 35 met the predefined criteria and 179 patients identified for the final analysis of which 149 are nonpharmacologically treated patients.
L1157: Assessment
L1158: A summary of response and 100-day survival are provided in Table 27. Please note that in Study OMS721-TMA-001, 100-day survival is defined as survival 100 days after diagnosis of HSCT-TMA. The definition of 100-day survival was not always provided for the literature-based review.
L1159: Table 27. Literature Based Historical Control TMA Response Rate
L1160: ParameterApplicantAnalysis of StudyTMA-001Literature-Based Historical ControlFDA Analysisof Study TMA-001

All PatientsNonpharmacologicallyTreated



Response rate(95% CI)17/28 (60.7%)(41% to 78%)58/179 (32%)(26% to 40%)36/149 (23.3%)(15.1% to 34.2%)12/28(24%, 63%)

L1161: Source: FDA Analysis
L1162: Abbreviation: CI, confidence interval; NA, not applicable
L1163: Assessment of survival (100-day survival) cannot be adequately assessed or compared due to lack of uniform definition. In the transplantation setting, 100 day survival often refers to survival 100 days after Day 0 of the hematopoietic stem cell transplantation.
L1164: Conclusion
L1165: In Study TMA-001, the Applicant used a 15% efficacy threshold and this was based on expert opinion only provided by the Applicant. There are significant limitations to literature-based review to support and verify this estimate. The Division sought expertise from Real World Evidence (RWE) internal FDA expert regarding quality of data. The limitations include unknown validity and accuracy of data, potential measurement errors associated with endpoints (response rates) and definition of 100-day survival across different publications, propensity score analysis performed on limited confounding variables, timing of treatment visits not standardized across different publications, study conducted using convenience samples and likely selection bias affects generalizability of the results. Therefore, the literature based review cannot be considered sufficient to Estimate response rate in an untreated group.
L1166: 6.3.3. Subject Matter Expert Analysis
L1167: Issue
L1168: The Applicant submitted a position paper by subject matter experts stating that the responses observed in Study OMS721-001 represent a true response to treatment with narsoplimab and that all 17 patients who Applicant identified are true responders.
L1169: The Division consulted with two subject matter experts to provide expert opinion.
L1170: Background
L1171: The Division sought input regarding the use of narsoplimab-wuug in patients with HSCT-TMA and requested input to address the following questions:
L1172: • Is there evidence of effectiveness for narsoplimab-wuug in the HSCT-TMA population taking into account the rarity of the disease, no FDA available therapies, trial limitations (single-arm trial), confounders in the interpretation of treatment effect of the efficacy endpoint (concomitant medications and illnesses), and discrepancies and inconsistencies in the application of the response definition for the subjects?• Is the response demonstrated in Study TMA-001 a significant change from what would be expected based upon the natural history and accepted standard of care treatment of this disease?
L1173: The Division provided the 28 narrative summaries, the clinical study report and background document to the SGEs. The SGEs provided summary response documents and the Division also met via teleconference with the SGEs to discuss responses.
L1174: Assessment
L1175: Response to the First Question
L1176: SGE number one reviewed all 28 cases and stated that 15/28 (%) patients appear to have a response to treatment and that all patients enrolled in the study were consistent with patients who had diagnosis of TMA.
L1177: SGE number two reviewed all 28 cases and stated that 16/28 (%) patients appeared to have a response to treatment and that all patients enrolled in the study were consistent with patients who had a diagnosis of TMA. Both SGE's did note that response was not always durable and that response consisted mostly of modest improvements in laboratory parameters of LDH or haptoglobin. Both consultants did comment that they would likely use this drug in the treatment of HSCT-TMA after failure of standard of conventional therapy (immunosuppressants modifications, off-label use of C5 inhibitors).:
L1178: Response to the Second Question
L1179: SGE 2 did comment that he felt that the survival observed in the study was a departure from what would be expected for this severely affected population. SGE 1 also commented that the response observed was an improvement from the known literature of response rates for patients with HSCT-TMA with the caveat that there has been difficulties in uniform consensus on diagnosis and response criteria. SGE 2 did note that the enrolled population appeared consistent with a severe population and that survival observed was better than what one would expect from similar population.
L1180: The table below provides a summary of response from the Applicant analysis, FDA analysis, literature review and the SGE review.

L1185: Conclusion
L1186: Overall, the SGE’s assessment of the individual narratives concluded that there was a response rate of 50% to 54%. Both SGEs concluded that the patients enrolled in the study are representative of a patient population with HSCT-TMA some who had severe HSCT-TMA. SGE 2 believed that the survival observed in the study was better than what one would expect from a similar population.
L1187: Both SGEs noted that the limitation of the study is the lack of a control arm but based on clinical experience, some patients had responses although not always robust responses. They also noted that most of the responses were primarily based on LDH and haptoglobin. Both SGEs stated they would likely use this medication in the clinical setting for patients with HSCT-TMA.
L1188: The SGE’s used clinical judgment to assess response rate but did not necessarily adhere to the protocol definition of response rate. The opinions provided by the SGE’s did not alter the conclusions of the clinical reviewers’ assessment of response rate.
L1189: 6.3.4. Pediatric Indication
L1190: Issue
L1191: The Applicant proposed to include a pediatric indication in the USPI but, there were no formal pediatric studies.
L1192: Background
L1193: The Applicant proposed to include a pediatric indication including ages 2 years of age and older. There are no approved therapies for HSCT-TMA in pediatric patients. The Applicant states that MASP-2 levels increase by age 2 and remain stable throughout childhood and are similar to adult levels. As stated in the Applicant's clinical overview the rationale for the proposal of a pediatric indication was based on dosing reasons of biological plausibility and consistency of the disease mechanism, role and levels of MASP-2 and lectin pathway activation across broad age groups, and the satisfactory therapeutic window enabled by weight-based dosing.
L1194: Assessment
L1195: No pediatric patients were treated in narsoplimab-wuug clinical trials. There was one 14-year-old adolescent patient treated on compassionate use that appeared to respond (based on clinical judgment) to narsoplimab-wuug. In the 90-day safety update it was reported four additional patients were treated with narsoplimab-wuug. Given the very limited experience of pediatric patients with narsoplimab-wuug the clinical review team does not agree with including a pediatric indication. It is not clear if pediatric patients will respond the same to narsoplimab-wuug as adults, in addition pediatric patients may have different comorbidities that may impact response. In addition, pediatric patients are more often transplanted for nonmalignant conditions compared to adults, and it's not clear how the indication for transplant may impact response to narsoplimab-wuug. Lastly, there is not sufficient safety data in the pediatric population.
L1196: Conclusion
L1197: The clinical review team believes that the sponsor must perform a pediatric assessment in order to support a pediatric indication and that full extrapolation from adults is unlikely to be acceptable.
L1198: 7. Risk and Risk Management
L1199: 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L1200: Preclinical safety of OMS721 was evaluated in a standard battery of nonclinical studies including safety pharmacology studies, repeat-dose toxicity studies in mice and monkeys for durations up to 4 and 26 weeks, respectively, reproductive toxicity studies in mice and rabbits, a juvenile toxicity study in mice, and a local tolerance study in rabbits. There were no nonclinical
L1201: safety issues of concern that substantially impacted clinical development as assessed by these studies.
L1202: Note that functional potency of OMS721, measured as inhibition of C4 and C3 activation in serum, is lower in nonclinical species than in humans; however, exposures evaluated in the pivotal toxicology studies achieved near- to complete inhibition of lectin-mediated complement activation, enabling a reasonable toxicological assessment of inhibiting the intended pharmacological target, MASP-2, by OMS721.
L1203: Cardiovascular and respiratory safety pharmacology assessments in monkeys and a CNS safety pharmacology assessment in mice showed no OMS721-related effects. In repeat-dose toxicology studies in mice and monkeys, minimal to mild subcutaneous injection site reactions were observed in both species. Sporadic increases were observed in AST and ALT in mice and monkeys in 28-day studies but were not present in the 26-week studies. Intravenous administration was also assessed, resulting in one instance of mild edema and minimal adventitial necrosis at the injection site, and one female that experienced pronounced decreases in total protein and albumin during treatment which became more severe during the recovery period. The adverse response of this female is of limited relevance to clinical risk, as the proposed clinical route of administration is subcutaneous, not intravenous. The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) identified in the 26-week monkey study was 300 mg/kg/week by the SC route (38-fold the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD)), while the NOAEL for the IV route was not established due to irreversible changes in protein and albumin levels at 150 mg/kg, the only IV dose studied (43-fold the MRHD).

L1210: 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L1211: The potential safety concerns were reviewed based on known information for other approved complement inhibitor therapies and literature review of patients with MASP-2 deficiency.
L1212: 7.3. Potential Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L1213: Narsoplimab-wuug is not approved or marketed in the United States or worldwide.
L1214: 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L1215: The Applicant’s safety database consisted of 6 completed or ongoing studies and a compassionate use program. The safety population consisted of all patients or subjects who received any amount of study drug.
L1216: For this review, the clinical review team considered the primary safety population to consist of the 28 patients with HSCT-TMA from Study OMS721-TMA-001 (TMA-001). The supportive safety population consisted of 256 patients from studies OMS721-NHV-001, OMS721-NHV-002, TMA-001 (excluding the 28 patients with HSCT-TMA included in the primary safety population), OMS-721-GNP-001, OMS721-IGA-001, OMS721-HUS-002, and the compassionate use program. Study OMS721-IGA-001 is ongoing and blinded and is minimally discussed in the safety review.
L1217: The data cutoff date for the safety assessments submitted in the initial BLA was December 20, 2019. The 90-Day safety update provided additional safety data collected from the 2 ongoing clinical trials from December 21, 2019 to August 31, 2020 (Studies OMS721 HUS-002 and OMS721-IGA-001) and safety data from compassionate use patients from December 21, 2019 to October 31, 2020.
L1218: Review of safety was based upon:
L1219: • CSR for studies• Protocol for studies• Data sets for the populations described above• Summary of clinical safety• 90-day safety update• Patient narratives• Case report forms
L1220: Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for adverse events (AEs) of interest, serious adverse events (SAEs), and deaths that occurred in the primary and supportive safety populations. Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) of special interest included infections.
L1221: No major issues were identified with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. The analysis dataset for adverse events (ADAE.xpt) data file was reviewed for accuracy of translation of the verbatim to preferred term by manual review. Based on this review a modified table (Table 30) was produced where additions of preferred terms were added based on the verbatim
L1222: term. With additions of preferred terms, the adverse event file was copied with the new preferred term. Adverse events were analyzed by MedDRA [version 22.0] preferred term. Similar preferred terms were grouped by standardized groupings of related preferred terms through FDA Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities queries (FMQs). Additional analyses were provided by the Clinical Data Scientist support team. The clinical review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a safety review.

L1227: 7.5. Adequacy of Clinical Safety Database
L1228: The clinical safety database is limited in patients with HSCT-TMA who were exposed but adequate given the rarity of HSCT-TMA and the availability of data from non-HSCT-TMA patients exposed to study drug to provide supportive safety data. In Study TMA-001, 28 patients with HSCT-TMA received at least one dose of narsoplimab-wuug. In total, 24 (85.7%) patients received weight-based dosing at 4 mg/kg IV, after protocol amendment 11.1, 4 (14.3%) patients received fixed doses of 370 mg IV. The mean duration of exposure was 6.7 weeks. A summary of exposure is shown in Table 31.
L1229: Duration of exposure in the supportive safety population can be found in Table 123.

L1234: 7.6. Safety Findings and Concerns Based on Review of Clinical Safety Database
L1235: 7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns, Study TMA-001
L1236: It is important to note that because the safety database is composed of only uncontrolled data, causality assessment of adverse reactions is very challenging. Therefore, all adverse events are assumed to be drug related adverse reactions for the purposes of this review. In Study TMA-001, infections, hemorrhage, diarrhea, neutropenia, pyrexia and vomiting were the most common adverse reactions. Infections were also the most common serious adverse reaction. In total, four (14%) of patients died during the AE evaluation period, including two (7%) due to adverse reactions of sepsis. One patient discontinued narsoplimab-wuug due to underlying disease progression which is likely unrelated to narsoplimab-wuug. The described safety profile is not unexpected in a complex, immunosuppressed, and ill patient population with multiple comorbidities, cytopenias, and who are receiving multiple concomitant medications.
L1237: A 90-day safety update was provided additional safety data on two ongoing clinical trials; HUS-002 and IGA-001, in addition to safety data from compassionate use patients with HSCT-TMA. Only SAEs, deaths, and discontinuations were reported. There were no events that changed the safety profile of narsoplimab-wuug.
L1238: 7.6.1.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event Summary, Study TMA-001
L1239: Grading of AEs was based on the National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events v.4.0. An overview of TEAEs that occurred in patients with HSCT-TMA during the AE evaluation period in Study TMA-001 is presented in Table 32. The AE evaluation period was defined from the time of informed consent to 37 days after the last narsoplimab-wuug administration. All but one patient experienced at least one TEAE. Most patients experienced a TEAE that was moderate or severe. In addition, the majority of patients had a SAE. In total, 4 patients had SAEs with fatal outcomes. There were low rates of AEs leading to discontinuation
L1240: or interruption of narsoplimab-wuug. See an overview of adverse events in the supportive safety population in Table 124.

L1247: 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Study TMA-001
L1248: In total, 16 (57.1%) of 28 patients died during Study TMA-001. Table 33 provides an overview of the deaths. An additional 2 patients died after withdrawing consent; the cause of death was unknown. Of the 11 (39.3%) patients who experienced a treatment-emergent death, the most common TEAE leading to death were infections: neutropenic sepsis, septic shock, gastrointestinal candidiasis, Escherichia sepsis, pneumonia, and pseudomonal sepsis. There were 4 (14.3%) patient deaths that occurred during the AE evaluation period as presented in Table 33, narratives are presented below.

L1260: 7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events, Study TMA-001
L1261: The most common (&gt;5%) SAEs reported by preferred terms in Study TMA-001 for HSCT-TMA patients included graft-versus-host-disease (GVHD) of the gastrointestinal tract, acute kidney injury, confusional state, acute respiratory failure, neutropenic sepsis, pulmonary edema, vomiting, and septic shock (Table 34). L1286: Clinical reviewer comment
L1287: SAEs occurred in approximately 61% of patients with HSCT-TMA treated with narsoplimab-wuug. This is not unexpected given patients were acutely ill at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis and with multiple comorbidities. Infections were most the most common treatment-emergent SAE and discussed more in Section 7.7.1 (key review issues). While GVHD was the next most common TEAE, this was unlikely related to narsoplimab-wuug, as this commonly occurs in the post-transplant setting. The clinical review team did not consider GVHD or malignancy to be an adverse reaction. However, given the lack of an adequate control, cannot fully determine attribution.
L1288: 7.6.1.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Events, Study TMA-001
L1289: One patient had an AE leading to discontinuation of study drug (Table 36). Patient (b) (6) received two doses of narsoplimab-wuug and then discontinued due to progressive general deterioration from failed HSCT, although the ADAE dataset reports the drug was discontinued due to worsening acute myeloid leukemia (AML). The patient died shortly after discontinuing narsoplimab-wuug. This TEAE was unlikely related to narsoplimab-wuug. See narrative in Section 17 Deaths.

L1295: In the supportive safety database, there were 6 discontinuations due to TEAEs. Table 127 lists the TEAEs leading to narsoplimab-wuug discontinuation. The Applicant highlighted three patients' discontinuations due to TEAEs as adverse events of special interest (AESI). The patient narratives describing TEAEs that led to premature narsoplimab-wuug discontinuation are described in the Section 17, this included a TEAE of supraventricular tachycardia (SVT) in a healthy volunteer, a TEAE of granulomatous orchitis in a patient with aHUS, and a TEAE of liver insufficiency in a patient with aHUS.
L1296: Clinical reviewer comment
L1297: There were low rates of drug discontinuations in the HSCT-TMA population despite consisting of an acutely ill population with multiple comorbidities. One patient in Study TMA-001 discontinued narsoplimab-wuug, this was likely related to the patient's underlying disease diagnosis of AML. In the supportive safety database one healthy volunteer discontinued narsoplimab-wuug due to an event of SVT. One other patient in the safety database developed SVT, this was a patient with HSCT-TMA, the patient did not discontinue therapy due to this event. It does not appear that SVT is an adverse reaction given low rates in the safety population. The TEAE of granulomatous orchitis and liver insufficiency are discussed in key review issues for safety.
L1298: 7.6.1.5. Dosage Interruptions Due to Adverse Events, Study TMA-001
L1299: Two patients (7.1%) with HSCT-TMA had a TEAE leading to drug interruption of narsoplimab-wuug (Table 37). Narratives are described below.
L1300: Patient (b) (6) is a 67-year-old female who received an allogeneic HSCT for AML. Following the diagnosis of HSCT-TMA the patient was enrolled on study and received narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV once a week. On study day 41 (b) (6) 7 days from the last dose of narsoplimab-wuug, the patient developed nausea, malaise, diarrhea, chills, and fever. She was hospitalized and found to be hypertensive and tachycardic. Blood culture was positive for E. coli. She was treated with antibiotics. During the event, narsoplimab-wuug was held for sepsis from (b) (6). Treatment resumed on (b) (6). Three days later on day 55 (b) (6) the patient developed grade 3 pyrexia and was hospitalized. Infectious work up was significant for a positive urine culture for Enterococcus faecium, weakly positive Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) PCR, and moderate bilateral pleural effusions. The patient was treated with
L1301: antibiotics. Narsoplimab-wuug was held during the event of pyrexia from (b) (6). On (b) (6) the patient received the eighth and final dose of narsoplimab-wuug.
L1302: Patient (b) (6) is a 48-year-old male who received an allogeneic HSCT for high grade myelodysplastic syndrome. The patient started narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV once a week on (b) (6) for HSCT-TMA. After receiving 5 doses of narsoplimab-wuug the study drug was interrupted (b) (6) for the events of pulmonary alveolar hemorrhage and myocardial infarction. On (b) (6) the patient had an ejection fraction of 50% and mild global left ventricular hypokinesia. On (b) (6) the patient developed hemoptysis and bronchoscopy was suggestive of diffuse alveolar hemorrhage (DAH). The patient was transferred to the intensive care unit (ICU) and found to have an elevated troponin T to 1194 ng/L (normal &lt;30 ng/L) and concern for type II myocardial infarction. The investigator believed the type II myocardial infarction was possibly due to post-dialysis hypotension. Both events resolved. The patient's TMA markers remained abnormal, and the patient subsequently withdrew from the study and died.

L1310: 7.6.1.6. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study TMA-001
L1311: The most common TEAEs (≥30%) in patients with HSCT-TMA in Study TMA-001 were infections, viral infections, hemorrhage, diarrhea, vomiting, neutropenia, pyrexia, fatigue, and GVHD. L1372: 7.6.1.7. Adverse Events of Special Interest, Study TMA-001
L1373: Infections
L1374: The Applicant identified infections as an adverse event of special interest. In patients with HSCT-TMA, 21 out of 28 (75%) of patients had a TEAE of infection (Tables 40 and 41). The most common types of infections included viral infections, sepsis, pneumonia and bacterial infections. Viral infections occurred in 36% of patients, the most common type of viral infection was cytomegalovirus. Serious TEAE infections occurred in 10 out of 28 (36%) of patients in the HSCT TMA population (Table 3 and Table 42). The most common serious TEAEs included sepsis, bacterial infections, viral infections and pneumonia. Safety analysis for infections are described below. Safety analysis for infections in the entire TMA population is described in Tables 129 and 130.

L1402: Source: Clinical reviewer [adae.xpt]
L1403: Immunogenicity
L1404: Per the Applicant’s CSR: 11 of 58 patients (19%) who were tested for antidrug antibody (ADA) were confirmed positive at one or more time points after narsoplimab-wuug administration (HSCT-TMA: 3/28, 10.7% and in aHUS or TTP: 8/30, 26.7%). Five patients reported hypersensitivity reactions, but all were attributable to other agents. One patient reported drug hypersensitivity to Tazocin (Patient (b) (6) ADA negative). Two patients reported allergic transfusion reactions (Patients (b) (6) both ADA positive). Two patients reported hypersensitivity reactions, both related to plasma therapy (Patients (b) (6); ADA positive and ADA negative, respectively). No apparent correlation of antibody development to altered clinical response or adverse events was observed.
L1405: 7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Risk
L1406: 7.7.1. Infections Were the Most Common TEAE Reported in Patients With HSCT-TMA
L1407: Issue
L1408: Infections were the most common TEAE reported in patients with HSCT-TMA. The Applicant has proposed to include infections in the Warnings and Precaution section of the USPI.
L1409: Background
L1410: Infections were identified as an adverse event of special interest by the Applicant. Narsoplimab-wuug is a human mAB that binds to and inhibits MASP-2. MASP-2 is an effector enzyme in the lectin complement pathway, which plays an important role in innate immunity (Fujita et al. 2004). Therefore, there could be a risk of increased infections in patients who receive narsoplimab-wuug based on its mechanism of action. While there are no approved drugs that specifically inhibit the lectin pathway, other drugs that inhibit different pathways of the complement (i.e., eculizumab and ravulizumab) are associated with an increased risk of infections, especially encapsulated organisms. In the literature, there are several reports of inherited MASP-2 deficiencies, a very rare primary immunodeficiency disorder. MASP-2 deficiency was first reported in 2003, which described a patient suffering from recurrent pneumococcal infections, as well as autoimmune and inflammatory conditions (Stengaard-Pedersen et al. 2003). MASP-2 deficiency is considered a primary immunodeficiency, associated with pyogenic infections, inflammatory lung disease, and autoimmunity (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). Since the first description, there have been several (although rare) individual case reports describing patients with MASP-2 deficiency. The clinical manifestations appear heterogenous ranging from recurrent pneumonia, cystic fibrosis, skin abscesses, colorectal cancer, pulmonary tuberculosis, and hepatocellular carcinoma (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). Recently, there were two reported cases of herpes simplex virus encephalitis reported in patients with MASP-2 deficiency (Bibert et al. 2019). MASP-2 deficiency has also been associated with an increased risk of fever and neutropenia in pediatric patients receiving chemotherapy (Brodzki et al. 2020). The p.D120G mutation, in a homozygous state is the primary cause of MASP-2 deficiency (Sokolowska et al. 2015). Yet, there has also been a significant number healthy individuals identified with MASP-2 deficiency with no overt symptoms, including patients with the p.D120G mutation (Thiel et al. 2007; Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). For example, homozygosity for p.D120G may occur in 7-15 out of every 10,000 individuals of European origin (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). It has been proposed that MASP-2 deficiency is a disease modifier rather than causing the disease (Thiel et al. 2007; Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). In addition, the lectin pathway is largely redundant in human defenses and MASP-2 deficiency classification as a primary immunodeficiency disorder has been questioned recently (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). Therefore, the clinical impact, including risk of infections of MASP-2 deficiency remains unclear.
L1411: Assessment
L1412: Infections were the most common TEAE, occurring in 21/28 (75%) of patients in Study TMA-001. The most common type of infection was viral, occurring in 10/28 (35.7%) of patients and the most frequently reported type of viral infection reported was CMV. Sepsis, pneumonia, bacterial infections, bacteremia, lower respiratory tract infections, upper respiratory tract infections and fungal infections occurred in &gt;10% of patients in HSCT-TMA (Table 129). No patients experienced an encapsulated organism infection of Neisseria meningitidis, Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib), or Streptococcus pneumoniae during the AE evaluation period. Of note, one patient had pneumococcal pneumonia, but this occurred in the post AE evaluation period. Serious infections occurred in 10/28 (35.7%) of patients. Neutropenic sepsis and septic shock occurred in two patients with HSCT-TMA. The following was reported in one patient; bacteremia, Escherichia sepsis, fungal infection, gastroenteritis, infection, viral pneumonia, viral respiratory tract infection, urosepsis, and pneumonia. In total, 7% of HSCT-TMA patients died due to infections.
L1413: Infections were also assessed in the integrated summary of safety (ISS) population which confirmed the safety findings from the primary safety population.
L1414: There was no comparator arm in Study TMA-001 so it is difficult to determine if infection rates are increased with narsoplimab-wuug compared to a control population. The Applicant provided data in response to an IR from two hospital centers that were sites in the trial (Table 44 and Table 45). The infection rates at the site for patients undergoing HSCT appear to be similar to what was reported in Study TMA-001, although interpretation is very difficult given this data were reported from only two centers and further data were not provided besides infection incidence post-allogeneic transplant (i.e., demographics, baseline characteristics, conditioning regimen, human leukocyte antigen mismatch, source of cells, immunosuppressants, comorbidities and severity of infections). In addition, these infection rates were not limited to patients with HSCT-TMA.

L1421: Source: Applicant's IR response
L1422: Abbreviations: ASST, Azienda Socio Sanitaria Territoriale; HSCT, hematopoietic stem cell transplant
L1423: Patients who undergo a HSCT are at increased risk for developing infections. This remains a common cause of morbidity and mortality in the post-transplant setting, due to a variety of factors including, but not limited to, the following: immunosuppressive therapies, multiple comorbidities, immune system dysfunction, neutropenia, and presence of central line. Therefore, patients treated with narsoplimab-wuug for HSCT-TMA are already at risk for serious infections. Based on the mechanism of action of narsoplimab-wuug, as a lectin complement inhibitor that inhibits MASP-2, it is possible that narsoplimab-wuug further increases the risk of infections as the lectin pathway is a component of the innate immune response and patients with inherited MASP-2 deficiency may be at increased risk of infections.
L1424: Conclusion
L1425: In conclusion, after review of the literature including reports of inherited MASP-2 deficiency, in addition to a significant number of patients experiencing serious or fatal infections in Study TMA-001, and the lack of comparative safety, an increased risk of serious infection with narsoplimab-wuug cannot be ruled out. Therefore, if the product were to be approved, serious infections should be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI. Serious infections should also be further monitored through a postmarketing requirement.
L1426: 7.7.2. Based on the Mechanism of Action of Narsoplimab-wuug of Inhibition of MASP-2, Narsoplimab-wuug May Increase Risk of Tuberculosis Infections
L1427: Issue
L1428: In clinical trials, one patient with aHUS developed a TEAE of granulomatous orchitis. Based on the mechanism of action of narsoplimab-wuug of inhibition of MASP-2, narsoplimab-wuug may increase risk of tuberculosis infections.
L1429: Background
L1430: One patient with aHUS developed granulomatous orchitis (biopsy proven) following three doses of narsoplimab-wuug. All cultures, including mycobacterium, were negative. The event resolved approximately 2 months after discontinuation of narsoplimab-wuug. Of note, this patient had a
L1431: previous mycobacterium infection several years prior to receiving narsoplimab-wuug. The full narrative can be found in Section 17.
L1432: Assessment
L1433: A review of the ADAE dataset did not reveal any additional TEAEs of orchitis, granuloma, or tuberculosis.
L1434: The Applicant and the clinical reviewer conducted a literature review to further understand whether inhibition of MASP-2 could increase the risk of tuberculosis. Of the 440 patients tested, two patients had pulmonary tuberculosis with MASP-2 deficiency, as did one healthy volunteer out of 276 healthy controls (Sokolowska et al. 2015). All three subjects had low MASP-2 concentrations and complex activities. A polymorphism of the MASP-2 gene was associated with nonsmoker's susceptibility to tuberculosis (Chen et al. 2015). Therefore, it is possible that narsoplimab-wuug, by blocking MASP-2, may have contributed to the development of granulomatous orchitis.
L1435: Given the TEAE of granulomatous orchitis in a patient treated with narsoplimab-wuug and literature suggesting that MASP-2 deficiency may increase risk of tuberculosis infection, there may be an increased risk of tuberculosis following treatment with narsoplimab-wuug. Therefore, the USPI should include in the Warnings and Precautions that a patient in clinical trials developed granulomatous orchitis and recommend that patients be assessed for risk factors of tuberculosis when considering the risk/benefit profile of narsoplimab-wuug.
L1436: Conclusion
L1437: In conclusion, one patient in clinical trials developed granulomatous orchitis. While no patients were reported to have a positive mycobacterium culture in Study MTA-001, the literature suggests that MASP-2 deficiency may increase susceptibility to tuberculosis. Therefore, one cannot rule out the possibility of contributing to the TEAE of granulomatous orchitis. Providers will be informed on this adverse reaction in the Warnings and Precautions of the USPI which will recommend assessing patients for risk factors. As only a small number of patients with HSCT-TMA have been treated with narsoplimab-wuug, we would also recommend that this risk be further assessed in a postmarketing requirement if the product were to be approved.
L1438: 7.7.3. Additional Analysis Conducted to Try to Determine Whether Narsoplimab-wuug Caused Drug Induced Liver Injury
L1439: Issue
L1440: In clinical trials, one patient developed grade 4 liver insufficiency.5 A clinical reviewer conducted an additional analysis to try to determine whether narsoplimab-wuug caused drug-induced liver injury.
L1441: Background
L1442: Patient (b) (6) was a 28-year-old female with a history of aHUS and end-stage renal disease requiring hemodialysis, who initiated narsoplimab-wuug per Study HUS-002. While receiving the narsoplimab-wuug the patient developed a TEAE of grade 4 hepatic failure. The hepatic failure occurred after eating a meal consisting of fish and while experiencing fever, hypotension, and hypoxia. In the safety narrative it was noted the patient received hydralazine from an unknown source, which was characterized as “overdosing on hydralazine.” The patient did not continue hydralazine during her hospitalization. Viral studies were negative and the patient did not have a history of consuming alcohol, acetaminophen, herbal supplements, or other over-the-counter medications. Narsoplimab-wuug was discontinued. The patient recovered from the liver insufficiency. A more detailed narrative can be found in Section 17.
L1443: The Agency sent an IR asking for additional information regarding the described TEAE. The investigator for the site was not the treating physician so limited details were provided. The interpretation that the event could have been related to hydralazine was based on the patient receiving hydralazine from an unknown source for hypertension and the liver insufficiency resolved in the hospital when the patient was not receiving hydralazine. The investigator also considered ischemic hepatitis to be a possible cause of the liver insufficiency. The differential for the etiology of ischemic hepatitis was the patient’s concurrent infection, hypoxia, and/or hypotension.
L1444: Assessment
L1445: The clinical reviewer reviewed the narrative to assess for causality. Temporally, narsoplimab-wuug could have contributed to the event of liver insufficiency as the patient was actively receiving narsoplimab-wuug when the event of grade 4 hepatic failure occurred and the TEAE
L1446: 5 Grade 4+, Severe: Raised serum aminotransferase or alkaline phosphatase levels and serum bilirubin \geq 2.5 mg/dL and at least one of the following:
L1447: Prolonged jaundice and symptoms beyond 3 months, or
L1448: Signs of hepatic decompensation (INR \geq 1.5, ascites, encephalopathy), or
L1449: Other organ failure believed to be related to drug induced liver injury
L1450: resolved with discontinuation of narsoplimab-wuug. At the same time, the patient did receive an unknown quantity of hydralazine. In the hydralazine USPI, hepatitis is listed as a rare adverse reaction. Hydralazine is described as a less concern for drug induced liver injury in the drug induced liver injury rank dataset. While rare, several case reports of hepatotoxicity associated with hydralazine described in the literature (Stumpf 1991; Harati et al. 2016; Sharma et al. 2018). The patient also had hypotension at the time of presentation of liver insufficiency, hepatic ischemia could have also contributed to the TEAE.
L1451: In clinical trials with narsoplimab-wuug, there were no other events of grade 4 hepatic failure or lesser extent of liver failure. The ISS ADAE dataset was reviewed for evidence of hepatobiliary injury. In patients with TMA (HSCT-TMA, aHUS, or TTP) 9 out of 64 (14.1%) patients experienced a TEAE of hepatobiliary injury (Table 46). The most common TEAE was blood alkaline phosphatase abnormal or increased, followed by alanine aminotransferase increased. All TEAEs were grade 1 or 2, except for a grade 3 serious TEAE of liver cirrhosis, grade 3 nonserious TEAE of hyperbilirubinemia, and a nonserious grade 3 TEAE of blood alkaline phosphatase increased. The safety narrative for patient (b) (6) was reviewed for the serious TEAE of liver cirrhosis. It is unlikely that the TEAE was related to narsoplimab-wuug as this patient had a history of chronic parenchymal liver disease and hyperammonemia. Two patients with IgA nephropathy experienced TEAEs of grade 2 transaminases increased; however, the study remains blinded to treatment assignment. No healthy volunteers had a TEAE related to hepatobiliary injury.

L1458: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; aHUS, atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome; HSCT-TMA, hematopoietic stem cell transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy; ISS, integrated summary of safety; N, number of subjects in treatment arm; n, number of subjects with adverse event; TEAE, treatment-emergent adverse events; TTP, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura
L1459: In a single-arm clinical trial it is difficult to determine if there is an association between hepatobiliary injury and narsoplimab-wuug given lack of comparative safety data. It is also particularly difficult given the complex nature of the patient population that may have additional
L1460: risk factors for hepatobiliary injury such as pre-existing hepatic injury, infections, ischemia, medications, veno-occlusive disease, and GVHD.
L1461: Conclusion
L1462: In clinical trials with narsoplimab-wuug there was one grade 4 TEAE of hepatic failure. This case was likely multifactorial as hydralazine and/or ischemic hepatitis could have contributed to the TEAE. It is difficult to determine causality as the event resolved with narsoplimab-wuug and hydralazine discontinuation, as well as treatment for the patient's hypotension. In addition, after careful review of hepatobiliary injury TEAEs across clinical trials there does not appear to be a safety signal. Therefore, the clinical reviewer does not recommend that hepatotoxicity be included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI should the product be approved; however, the clinical reviewer does recommend that hepatobiliary injury be further assessed in future post-marketing assessments.
L1463: 7.7.4. Second Most Common TEAE (FMQ) Was Hemorrhage
L1464: Issue
L1465: The second most common TEAE (FMQ) was hemorrhage.
L1466: Background
L1467: Given that hemorrhage was the second most common TEAE observed in Study TMA-001, the Agency asked the Applicant if they had data regarding the frequency of hemorrhage/bleeding events in the post-transplant setting as a comparison. The Applicant did not have access to this data. The Agency reviewed the hemorrhage TEAEs in the single arm trial of Study TMA-001 and the ISS dataset.
L1468: Assessment
L1469: During the FMQ analysis the second most common TEAE in Study TMA-001 was hemorrhage. Hemorrhage occurred in 12/28 (42.9%) of patients with HSCT-TMA (Table 47). Most TEAEs were grade 1 or 2. Serious TEAEs of hemorrhage occurred in 2/28 (7.1%) of patients with HSCT-TMA, this included intracranial hemorrhage and pulmonary alveolar hemorrhage.
L1470: In the ISS safety population, 8 out of 36 (22.2%) patients with either aHUS or TTP experienced a TEAE of hemorrhage (FMQ). Four out of 29 (13.8%) patients with IgA nephropathy (from study GNP-001) had a TEAE of hemorrhage, 3 of those patients were receiving narsoplimab-wuug and 1 was receiving placebo. In total, 3 healthy volunteers had TEAEs of hemorrhage, two patients were receiving narsoplimab-wuug had had a contusion and one was receiving placebo and had a vessel site puncture site hematoma.
L1471: In the ISS safety population, there were two patients with serious TEAEs of hemorrhage. This included one patient with aHUS treated on HUS-002 with a fatal event of cerebral hematoma, this patient had a recent history of subdural hemorrhage and thrombocytopenia prior to the event,
L1472: and thrombocytopenia was ongoing at the time of the cerebral hemorrhage. The other patient also had aHUS and was treated on TMA-001 and had a grade 4 TEAE of postprocedural hemorrhage following a kidney biopsy.
L1473: In reviewing the literature, hemorrhage does not seem to be described as a component of the phenotype of patients with inherited MASP-2 deficiency.

L1476: Source: Clinical reviewer; ADAE.xpt
L1477: Abbreviations: HSCT-TMA, hematopoietic stem cell transplant-associated thrombotic microangiopathy
L1478: Conclusion
L1479: Hemorrhage is known to occur in patients in the post-transplant setting due to a variety of factors, most notably for thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy, procedures and liver dysfunction and HSCT-TMA. Causality is difficult to assess, given that Study TMA-001 was a single arm trial and all patients had thrombocytopenia. In addition, there may be deficiencies in the coagulation cascade and fibrinogen levels in this seriously ill population. In reviewing the ISS safety database there does not appear to be an increased risk of hemorrhage due to narsoplimab-wuug, but this database is small and patients with other forms of TMA had thrombocytopenia. Hemorrhage will be included in the Adverse Reactions section of the USPI given the high frequency of bleeding events.
L1480: 7.7.5. Autoimmunity Has Rarely Been Associated With MASP-2 Deficiency; Given That Narsoplimab-wuug Inhibits MASP-2, There Is a Theoretical Risk of Autoimmunity
L1481: Issue
L1482: Autoimmunity has rarely been associated with MASP-2 deficiency. Given that narsoplimab-wuug inhibits MASP-2, there is a theoretical risk of autoimmunity.
L1483: Background
L1484: Complement has an important role in autoimmune disease, mostly associated with components of the classical complement pathway. It is not clear if there is an association between deficiencies in components of the lectin pathway and autoimmune disease development. MASP-2 deficiency has been described as a primary immunodeficiency disorder characterized by infections, inflammatory lung disease and autoimmunity (Bousfiha et al. 2018; Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). In the novel case of MASP-2 deficiency the patient had autoimmune manifestations but was also noted to have low levels of C1q, C3, and C4 (Garcia-Laorden et al. 2020). A case report describing a patient with Crohn’s disease and MASP-2 deficiency was reported (Hejazi and Hasosah 2019).
L1485: Assessment
L1486: The entire ISS dataset was assessed for autoimmune diseases. There were no apparent autoimmune diseases that occurred during time period reviewed for the study. Table 48 describes preferred terms captured under immune system disorders (captured all TEAEs in the AE and post AE evaluation period). Most TEAEs were related to GVHD, which is not unexpected in a post-transplant patient population. Hypersensitivity reactions occurred in three patients and all were reported as unrelated to narsoplimab-wuug. Two patients had an allergic reaction to plasma therapy and one had an allergic reaction to Tazocin.
