L2065: Assessment of the Covariate Analysis
L2066: • The PK parameters from the final model and from the nonparametric bootstrap were estimated with acceptable precision, except for the estimated effect of health status on K_m. Deletion of health status as a covariate on K_m did not change the objective function value (OFV), with an OFV decrease of 0.7 points. The unexplained BSV on K_m remained unchanged as well, indicating that health status is likely not a relevant covariate on K_m. The effect of health status on V_{max} was found not statistically significant as well, after backward deletion of health status as a covariate on V_{max} from the Applicant full covariate model. The deletion of health status as a covariate on V_{max} increased the objective function by 2.8 points. The unexplained BSV on V_{max} remained unchanged.• Among the PK parameters that are affected by the identified covariates, the ETA shrinkage was acceptable for reliable assessment of covariate effects. According to the base PK model, the ETA shrinkage was low and ranged from 3.7% to 17.7% for CL, V_1 and Q. The ETA shrinkage for the remaining parameters was relatively higher for V_2 (32.5%), K_m (41%) and V_{max} (38.7%), which makes the graphical assessment of covariate on these parameters less reliable.• Individual dose was found to be a statistically significant covariate on the central volume of distribution (V_1) and was considered directly related to body weight as narsoplimab-wuug dosing was a weight-based dosing. The reviewer's evaluation of body weight (included as power model standardized to 77.2 kg) instead of dose as covariate on V_1 resulted in an increased OFV (by 34 points).
L2067: • The presence of ADA was estimated to increase V_{max} by 143% according to the popPK model, which is predicted to result at steady-state exposure in about 22% decrease in AUC_{ss} and 52% decrease in C_{min,ss}.• PLT and more specifically plasma exchange (PE) is expected to remove large molecules such as proteins and monoclonal antibodies from the systemic circulation. Five patients received PE during narsoplimab-wuug treatment, and were used to evaluate the effect of PE on narsoplimab-wuug PK. The median duration of PE in these 5 patients was 1.8 h (ranging from 1.4 h to 2.9 h).
L2068: The reviewer's reassessment of the effect of PE on narsoplimab-wuug CL, after appropriate modification and rearrangement of the PK dataset, found that PE increased narsoplimab-wuug CL by an average of 47% (with an OFV decrease of 4.5 for 1 degree of freedom). However, the estimated effect on narsoplimab-wuug CL could not be precisely estimated, with a 95% CI of the mean effect ranging from 24% to 124% (determined from bootstrap, n=200). In addition, the covariate effect was sensitive to the initial value in the PK model.
L2069: The difficulty in reliably estimating the effect of PE on CL using the population PK model is most likely related to the lack of informative PK data. There was no PK sampling performed concurrently immediately pre- and post-PE amongst the 5 patients who received PE during narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The potential estimated effect of PE on the PK is likely driven by 3 of the 5 patients, who had post-PE PK samples collected (Figure 28). Besides, the duration of the procedure was not found to impact the estimated effect.

L2074: Assessment of the Need for Supplemental 2 mg/kg Narsoplimab-wuug Dose After Plasma Exchange
L2075: According to current PK data and the reviewer's PK simulations from the modified PK model (with its known limitations), 1) PE does not seem to have a relevant effect on narsoplimab-wuug PK despite the average 47% effect on CL and therefore 2) a supplemental narsoplimab-wuug of 2 mg/kg immediately post-PE does not seem to be needed to correct narsoplimab-wuug exposures.
L2076: In Study TMA-001, patients who received PLT during treatment received a supplemental narsoplimab-wuug dose of about 2 mg/kg after PLT. Two patients with HSCT-TMA, in Study TMA-001, received plasma exchange (b) (6) and plasma infusion (b) (6) before narsoplimab-wuug dose at the day of treatment initiation. These two patients would theoretically not need supplemental doses to correct for potential decrease in exposure. However, these patients received either a single supplemental dose (b) (6) or multiple supplemental doses (b) (6) in addition to unscheduled full narsoplimab-wuug doses during treatment (Figure 29), without clinical justification by the Applicant for these supplemental doses and unscheduled doses.

L2081: The effect of PLT on narsoplimab-wuug CL and its overall exposure was not investigated in a dedicated study, and no justification for the selected supplemental narsoplimab-wuug dose of 2 mg/kg as well as the frequency of the supplemental dose administration was provided by the Applicant. Based on our information request, the Applicant estimated that PLT increased narsoplimab-wuug CL by 37.1% (using popPK modeling) and concluded that due to the modest effect size and short duration of PLT, no clinically meaningful effects of PLT on narsoplimab-wuug exposure could be identified that would support supplemental doses following plasma exchange.
L2082: The reviewer's assessment of the effect of PE on narsoplimab-wuug CL, found that PE increased narsoplimab-wuug CL by an average of 47% during an average 2-hour PE window. However,
L2083: the estimated effect on narsoplimab-wuug CL could not be precisely estimated, with a 95% CI of the mean effect ranging from 24% to 124%. Although the PE effect on CL could not be reliably estimated (likely due to the lack of informative PK data), PK simulations were performed to evaluate the need for an additional supplemental dose of 2 mg/kg after PE to correct for potential change in narsoplimab-wuug exposure. Different scenarios and frequencies of PE were simulated during a dosing interval (i.e., one PE on Day 1, two PE on Day 1 and Day 2, two PE on Day 1 and Day 4, two PE on Day 5 and Day 6, three PE on Day 1, Day 3 and Day 6 post-Narsoplimab-wuug single dose administration, or PE on Day 7 before the second narsoplimab-wuug dose administration).
L2084: Single 4 mg/kg dose simulations using the modified PK model (with the estimated average 47% effect of PE on CL) failed to show difference in narsoplimab-wuug PK in a 70 kg subject without PE or receiving a 2-hour PE up to three times during a dosing interval of 7 days (Figure 30, red or blue lines and shaded areas).
L2085: The simulated administration of a supplemental dose of 2 mg/kg immediately after each 2-hour PE event resulted generally in higher narsoplimab-wuug PK exposure and trough concentrations (green line and shaded area) compared to the absence of supplemental dose, particularly in case of more than one PE or PE occurring towards the end of dosing interval. In agreement with the observed PK data from the clinical studies, a peak of narsoplimab-wuug concentration occurs after each supplement narsoplimab-wuug dose post-PE, and these predicted peak concentrations were generally within the range of C_{max} expected from the conventional scheduled dose of 4 mg/kg.

L2095: 14.3.3. Applicant's Population PK-PD (C4d Inhibition) Analysis
L2096: Narsoplimab-wuug is a human IgG4 monoclonal antibody that binds to and inhibits MASP-2. Inhibition of ex-vivo lectin-mediated C4d (complement component 4d) formation was assessed, as a PD biomarker of the mechanism of action, and the PK/C4d relationship across all studies was analyzed. The ex-vivo assay of C4d inhibition was used to justify the selection of 4 mg/kg IV dosing in the Phase 2 studies as it was expected to maintain mean lectin pathway inhibition &gt;80% for about 168 hours, the proposed dosing interval. However, C4d inhibition was not used as an efficacy endpoint in evaluating treatment response.
L2097: The majority of the C4d inhibition records had a time-matched PK observation, and the observed PK data were used to characterize C4d inhibition, rather than model predicted concentrations. PK concentrations below the limit of quantification were set to lower limit of quantification (LLOQ)/2 (25 ng/mL). The C4d assay depends on the availability of mannan-binding lectin. In 4 subjects
L2098: (b) (6) the baseline mannan-binding lectin levels were below the limit of quantification (&lt;35.2 ng/mL). These subjects were excluded from the subsequent PK/PD model development, as the C4d assay depends on the availability of mannan-binding lectin. Some C4d inhibition values were measured outside of the stability window of 836 days. These records were flagged and tested for exclusion during model development. Recorded C4d values were capped at -50% or +200% to reduce the huge variability in these data and improve the PK-
L2099: PD model stability. C4d observations ranged from -1359% to +2430%, however large values were relatively rare (Figure 31).

L2102: Source: Applicant's Population PK Report, Figure 29, page 80
L2103: Base PK-PD Model
L2104: A direct link E_{max} model was used to describe the relationship between narsoplimab-wuug concentrations and C4d inhibition. The E_{max} model was parameterized in terms of baseline C4d inhibition at zero narsoplimab-wuug concentration (E_0), maximum C4d inhibition response (E_{max}), and narsoplimab-wuug concentration required to achieve half (50%) of the maximum C4d inhibition effect or change from baseline (EC_{50}). A Hill coefficient or shape parameter (\Gamma) was estimated to be close to 1 and subsequently fixed to 1. BSV was estimated on all parameters, except for \Gamma, with an additive random effect of E_0 and E_{max} and exponential random effect on EC_{50}. The residual error model consisted of an additive error model. The parameter estimates from the base PK-PD model are listed below in Table 107.

L2112: Covariates Analysis
L2113: A stepwise covariate modeling procedure was used in order to build the covariate model, with a significance level of 0.05 for the forward inclusion and 0.001 for the backward deletion step. Tested potential covariates were gender, patient or health status, diseases, race, ethnicity, ADA status, age, body weight, baseline albumin, creatinine concentration, and platelet counts
L2114: The following covariates were included in the final model:
L2115: • Body weight on E_{max}, as power function centered at 77.2 kg• Body weight on E_0, as proportional linear function centered at 77.2 kg• Baseline albumin on E_0, as power function centered at 36 g/L• Health status on E_0, as percent difference using proportional linear model• IgAN (IgA Nephropathy) disease on E_0, as percent difference using proportional linear model
L2116: Final Model
L2117: The parameter estimates from the final PK-PD model are listed in Table 108, and the model-predicted C4d inhibition versus narsoplimab-wuug concentrations is presented in Figure 32. According to the model, Narsoplimab-wuug plasma concentration responsible for 50% of the maximum C4d inhibition effect (EC_{50}) was estimated to be 664 ng/mL (0.664 µg/mL). Typical parameters were estimated with good precision while covariate effect parameters had standard errors up to 40% and 57.9% in the case of albumin on E_0. The ETA shrinkage ranged from 10.4 to 11.1% and the residual error (Epsilon) shrinkage was 4.2%.
L2118: The goodness-of-fit plots from the final PK-PD model, are shown in Figure 33. The plots of the observed C4d inhibition versus the population predicted or the individual predicted C4d inhibition show random normal scatter around the identity lines, indicating absence of systematic bias. The population and individual conditionally weighted residuals versus population predicted C4d inhibition also show random normal scatter around zero with no specific trend, indicating no apparent model misspecifications.

L2136: The nonparametric bootstrap (500 replicates), to assess the robustness of the final PK model, found that the deviation between the final model parameter estimates and the mean bootstrap estimates were small (-16.8% to 0%). The bootstrap 95% CI were close to the original CIs based on standard errors, except for the estimated effects of baseline albumin and IgAN disease as covariates on E_0 for which the 95% CI included 0 (Table 109).

L2144: In Table 109, the Applicant's reported BSV values are the variances and not the standard deviations. The reported corr(EC50) is not the correlation but the actual covariance between EC50 and Emax random effects.
L2145: 14.3.4. Reviewer's Assessment on the PK-PD Model
L2146: Adequacy of the PK-PD Model in Describing PD Data
L2147: • The Applicant's PK-PD model adequately describes the measured C4d inhibition from the narsoplimab-wuug clinical studies.
• The Applicant's PK-PD modeling was not a simultaneous or sequential modeling PK-PD approach. The measured PK data were used directly to characterize the PD model, since the majority of the C4d inhibition records had a time-matched PK observation (the below quantification limit PK values were set to LLOQ/2). The reviewer re-evaluated the PK-PD model using a sequential PK-PD modeling approach, where the individual predicted PK concentrations (estimated using the developed PK model) were used to characterize the PD model. The PK-PD model parameters using either the observed PK data (i.e.,
L2148: Applicant's model) or the individual predicted PK data (i.e., reviewer's model) were comparable, when considering the base and the full covariate model.
L2149: • The recorded C4d inhibition data were highly variable, and the Applicant truncated the C4d values at -50% or +200% to reduce the huge variability in the data and improve the PK-PD model stability. The reviewer re-evaluated the PK-PD model using after considering the C4d inhibition values between 0 and 100% only. The resulting PK-PD model parameter estimates were comparable to those observed from the Applicant's PK-PD model.• There was no observed delay in the C4d inhibition response, with maximum C4d inhibition occurring around peak narsoplimab-wuug concentrations. In addition, the evaluation of an effect compartment in the PK-PD model did not improve the model.
L2150: Assessment of the Covariate Analysis
L2151: • Among the PD model parameters that are affected by the identified covariates, the ETA shrinkage was acceptable for reliable assessment of covariate effects. According to the Applicant's base PK-PD model, the ETA shrinkage was 7.8%, 12.5%, and 25.6% for E_0, EC_{50} and E_{max}, respectively.• The PD parameters from the Applicant's final PK-PD model and from the nonparametric bootstrap were estimated with acceptable precision, except for the estimated effect of baseline albumin and IgAN disease on E_0, with the bootstrap 95% CI including 0. The precision around the estimated mean effect of body weight on E_0 and E_{max} was relatively high (&gt;30%).
L2152: The evaluation of the individual ETA for E_0 versus covariate plot from the base model did not show a trend between E_0 and baseline albumin. No clinical justification was provided for including the effect of albumin on the baseline levels of C4d inhibition (E_0). The deletion of baseline albumin as a covariate on E_0 from the final model did not result in a statistically significant change of the OFV at a 0.001 significance level (OFV increased by 5.8 points for 1 degree of freedom).
L2153: The univariate deletion of IgAN or health status as a covariate on E_0 from the final model resulted in a statistically significant change of the OFV at a 0.001 significance level (OFV increased by 16.8 and 18.5 points for 1 degree of freedom, respectively). The covariate plot from the base model showed a slightly lower median E_0 in healthy subjects and IgAN patients compared to the other disease conditions. However, these differences in E_0 were not expected to be clinically relevant given the high and overlapping variability in E_0 between healthy subjects and patients, as well as between the various disease conditions among patients. In addition, if we consider the mechanism of action of the drug, baseline C4d inhibition (i.e., in the absence of drug) would be anticipated to be either lower in patients compared to healthy subjects or comparable to healthy subject due to other standard of care procedure pre-enrollment.
L2154: Although, body weight remained statistically significant covariates on E_0 and E_{max}, after univariate deletion from the Applicant's full covariate model, the ETA versus covariate plots for E_0 and E_{max} did not show a trend with body weight. In addition, the estimated effect of body
L2155: weight on E_0 and E_{max} has low precision (35.4% and 39.7% respectively) and were close to zero. Therefore, body weight is not expected to be clinically relevant covariate on E_0 and E_{max}.
L2156: Finally, the univariate covariate analysis using the Applicant's base model found that only health status on E_0 and body weight on E_{max} were statistically significant at a 0.05 significance level (decrease in OFV by -4.2 and -10.3 for 1degree of freedom, respectively).
L2157: • Covariate analysis performed on the reviewer's PK-PD model, which linked the individual predicted PK concentrations (from the developed PK model) instead to the current observed concentrations to C4d inhibition, found that baseline albumin on E_0 and body weight on E_{max} were not statistically significant covariates at a 0.001 significance level after univariate deletion from the full covariate model (in agreement with the ETA versus covariate plots showing the absence of trends).
L2158: This was also in agreement with the univariate covariate analysis using the reviewer's base model, which found that only health status on E_0 and body weight on E_{max} were statistically significant at a 0.05 significance level (decrease in OFV by -4.9 and -6.4 for 1degree of freedom, respectively), but were not statistically significant at significance level &lt;0.01.
L2159: In conclusion, the investigated covariates on the Applicant's and the reviewer's base PK-PD models are not considered clinically relevant due to:
L2160: • The weak statistical significance of the included covariates in the Applicant's and the reviewer's full covariate PK-PD models.
• The absence of a relationship between the PD parameters and the covariates from the ETA versus covariate plots from the Applicant's and the reviewer's base PK-PD models.
• The absence of clear physiological relevance of these covariates.

L2165: Note: Estimates for the between-subject variability were reported as the square root of variance and expressed as %CV for the log-normally distributed parameter EC_{50}. %RSE is the relative standard error expressed in percentage. The E_{max} model for the PD response (the measured C4d inhibition in %) was model as:
L2166: Response = E_0 + \frac{(E_{max} - E_0) \times [Narsoplimab\ concentration]^\gamma}{EC_{50}^\gamma + [Narsoplimab\ concentration]^\gamma}
L2167: where the hill coefficient (\gamma) was fixed to 1. The reviewer's assessment of an alternative E_{max} model with a proportional increase in maximum effect from baseline (E_0) did not improve the fit.
L2168: Abbreviation: CV, coefficient of variation; PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; RSE, relative standard error
L2169: Association between narsoplimab-wuug concentrations and the level of C4d inhibition during narsoplimab-wuug treatment in patients with HSCT-TMA amongst treatment responders and non-responders
L2170: The ex-vivo assay of C4d inhibition was used to justify the selection of 4 mg/kg IV dosing in Phase 2 studies as it was expected to maintain mean lectin pathway inhibition &gt;80% for about 168 hours, the proposed dosing interval. However, C4d inhibition was not used as marker of efficacy or treatment response in the study.
L2171: However, no association was observed between narsoplimab-wuug exposure or trough concentrations or the extent of C4d inhibition and the response to narsoplimab-wuug treatment in patients with HSCT-TMA. As depicted from Figure 34, almost all non-responder to narsoplimab-wuug treatment had consistently comparable or higher exposure than treatment responders, with trough narsoplimab-wuug concentrations consistently above IC80 for the majority of non-responders. Conversely, amongst the treatment responders, many patients had their trough narsoplimab-wuug concentrations just above IC50 for C4d inhibition.

L2176: Amongst the patients who responded to narsoplimab-wuug treatment, patient (b) (6) received a single plasma infusion before treatment initiation and one supplemental dose of 2 mg/kg on the
L2177: second week of treatment (without justification and regardless of the absence of PLT, Figure 29). Patient (b) (6) received a single PE before treatment initiation and 21 supplemental doses of 2 mg/kg (until day 42), followed by 9 unscheduled doses of 5 mg/kg between day 56 and day 109 (in addition to weekly 5 mg/kg narsoplimab-wuug doses, Figure 29). Amongst the patients who did not respond to narsoplimab-wuug treatment, patient (b) (6) received two PE on day 10 and day 11 with one supplemental narsoplimab-wuug dose of 2 mg/kg post-PE.
L2178: PK simulations were performed to predict the expected narsoplimab-wuug PK profiles from these 3 patients in the absence of supplemental and unscheduled doses. The PK simulations showed that the PK profiles and narsoplimab-wuug trough concentrations are expected to remain above IC80 during treatment even in the absence of supplemental and unscheduled doses.

L2184: 14.4. Summary of Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L2185: Concentrations of narsoplimab-wuug in human serum were determined using a validated ELISA method at the Bioanalytical Laboratory of (b) (4). A Nunc 96 well ELISA plate was coated with MASP-2A and incubated overnight at 4 °C. After incubation, the plates were washed and blocked. Diluted narsoplimab-wuug-containing serum samples were transferred to the coated plate and incubated for 2 hours at room temperature. Mouse antihuman IgG4 horseradish peroxidase-conjugated antibodies were added to the wells after washing. The detection substrate TMBred was added after a final wash and detection of TMBox was used to determine narsoplimab-wuug concentrations. The review team has determined that the method validation and sample analysis are acceptable.

L2203: 16. Efficacy: Additional Information and Assessment
L2204: Patient Narratives from Study TMA-001
L2205: Patient (b) (6)
L2206: The patient was a 48-year-old Caucasian male who received a matched unrelated donor transplant after failed induction for biphenotypic acute leukemia. He had complicated post-HSCT course. On Day 5 post-HSCT, he developed a productive cough with fever and diarrhea. He was treated empirically with gentamycin and tazocin. Blood cultures were positive for Streptococcus oralis. Sputum culture grew Candida krusei. On Day 6, his creatinine increased and cyclosporin dose was reduced. On Day 9, he developed hypoxia requiring supplemental oxygen, anti-infectives were changed to meropenem and caspofungin. On Day 11, his creatinine continued to increase, believed to be related to sepsis and intravascular fluid depletion. The mucositis and pneumonia slowly improved allowing pain medications and oxygen to be decreased. Neutrophil engraftment occurred on approximately Day 21.
L2207: On day 17, the patient had fever and diarrhea. A diagnosis of liver GVHD was made and the patient was started on methylprednisolone. On day 18, vancomycin was started. The patient improved but developed posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome (PRES) on day 22 and cyclosporin was stopped. It was at this time the diagnosis of HSCT-TMA was made on day 23. The TMA diagnosis was based on elevated lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), schistocytes, undetectable haptoglobin, and an elevated creatinine. Laboratory markers at the time of study entry and shown in Table 114. On day 33, the patient received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug, the following day the investigator was informed the patient had E. coli 0157. The patient was asymptomatic at that time and therefore no treatment was given. In total, the patient received 8 weekly doses of narsoplimab-wuug. The patient continued to have neutropenia and thrombocytopenia during treatment, this was thought to be secondary to mycophenolate mofetil and valganciclovir, or graft failure. The patient did not engraft platelets and remained platelet and blood transfusion dependent. The patient met a response based on LDH and renal response. Response based on TMA markers was noted 15 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. The patient ultimately died on day 215 due to neutropenic sepsis.
L2208: Reviewer comment
L2209: This is a complex patient with recent diagnosis of PRES, liver GVHD, and infections in relation to HSCT-TMA. While GVHD and infections are risk factors for HSCT-TMA often, the first step in treatment of HSCT-TMA is to treat the underlying cause and/or withdrawal or reduction of calcineurin inhibitors (CNIs) and sirolimus (Rosenthal 2016; Khosla et al. 2018; Elemary et al. 2019). Therefore, the impact of treating these risk factors of liver GVHD and infections make it difficult to interpret if the patient's clinical improvement was due to narsoplimab-wuug or treating the underlying cause. The patient also had cyclosporin discontinued at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis, 10 days before starting narsoplimab-wuug. The Bone Marrow Transplant Clinical Trials Network Toxicity Committee consensus summary recommended CNI cessation as the first line management (Ho et al. 2005; Epperla et al. 2020). Although there is debate if
L2210: removal of CNIs and sirolimus improves HSCT-TMA, as some recent large cohort. studies have not demonstrated a benefit (Epperla et al. 2020).
L2211: The confounders described above make the interpretation of narsoplimab-wuug treatment effect difficult, the clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as he did not meet all three components of the predefined response definition. While the reviewer understands the patient never engrafted platelets, this is a critical component of the response definition and therefore response is less compelling. When looking at the patient's TMA markers it is quite apparent that the haptoglobin improved after starting narsoplimab-wuug, this could be a sign of response to narsoplimab-wuug, but this was not included in the primary composite endpoint definition and how this translates to clinical benefit is not clear. While the LDH improved on narsoplimab-wuug, appears to have been down trending prior to narsoplimab-wuug administration, see Figure 35 below. Also, the patient never achieved blood transfusion freedom. Of note, the patient was also reported to have a positive E. coli 0157. The clinical reviewer was not able to confirm this as it was stated in the narrative, but not documented elsewhere. E. coli 0157 can cause typical hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS), another form of TMA.

L2221: Patient (b) (6)
L2222: The patient was a 58-year-old Asian male with a history of myelodysplastic syndrome that evolved into acute myeloid leukemia (AML). After achieving a complete remission, he had a matched related HSCT. Following transplant, the patient developed mucositis requiring total parenteral nutrition. On day 5, his creatinine increased. He developed possible typhlitis and cefepime and metronidazole was started, with amphotericin B added shortly after. He was requiring both blood and platelet transfusions. On day 12, he required intubation for respiratory distress and was diagnosed with diffuse alveolar hemorrhage (DAH). Vancomycin and steroids
L2223: were added to his treatment regimen. Fungal pneumonia was also a concern. He was diagnosed with cytomegalovirus (CMV) reactivation on day 15 and was started on ganciclovir. Neutrophil engraftment occurred on day 15, the patient never engrafted platelets. He developed fluid overload and was started on dialysis. He was extubated on day 19. Tacrolimus and sirolimus was temporarily discontinued from day 15 and day 21, respectively.
L2224: The patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 21, laboratory markers supporting this diagnosis were schistocytes, thrombocytopenia, and elevated LDH. Laboratory markers at time of study entry are shown in Table 115. The patient received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 36. He received 8 doses total. Response based on TMA markers was noted 7 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug, the patient met response based on LDH and renal response. He never engrafted platelets.
L2225: The patient was discharged home, but subsequently readmitted on day 39 for GI-GVHD and persistent pancytopenia. Tacrolimus, solomedrol, basiliximab, ruxolitinib, and budesonide were added to the patient's treatment plan. On day 48, encephalopathy was noted and tacrolimus was discontinued. On day 54, acyclovir was held for worsening renal function. The patient suffered from encephalopathy, sepsis and additional viral infections. Graft failure was noted on day 96 and the patient died on day 100 due to septic shock.
L2226: Reviewers comments
L2227: This patient had a complicated post-HSCT course. Around the time of his HSCT-TMA diagnosis the patient was also diagnosed with possible pneumonia, typhlitis and GVHD. The patient was subsequently treated with antibiotics, antivirals and antifungal agents, in addition to steroids for the GVHD. The treatment of these underlying risk factors may impact the interpretability of narsoplimab-wuug.
L2228: While the confounders described above make the interpretation of narsoplimab-wuug treatment effect difficult, the clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as he did not meet all three components of the predefined response definition. While the reviewer understands the patient never engrafted platelets, this is a critical component of the response definition and therefore response cannot be substantially determined. In addition, the patient was noted to have renal response, but never had a doubling of creatinine from pretransplant level. In fact, at the time of screening, the patient's serum creatinine was 0.76 mg/dL which was down trending (see Figure 36), while the creatinine further decreased on therapy, this magnitude of change is difficult to interpret. The patient did have a reduction in LDH, although the LDH was already down trending prior to narsoplimab-wuug administration (see Figure 36). Of note, the patient had a low haptoglobin that drastically increased to normal while receiving therapy, this may provide evidence of narsoplimab-wuug effectiveness, but it is unknown how this translates to clinical benefit. It is worth noting that while it is unlikely the patient had TTP, and ADAMTS13 was not documented, this is typically standard to rule out when considering TMA.

L2239: Patient
L2240: (b) (6)
L2241: The patient was a 22-year-old Hispanic female with a history of relapsed B-acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL) who received a matched sibling HSCT. On day 18, she developed a persistent fever, she was treated with micafungin, cefepime and vancomycin. Her blood cultures were positive for Haemophilus influenzae. On day 20, she was diagnosed with GI-GVHD and was treated with solomedrol, basiliximab, and budesonide. Day 24 bronchoscopy was significant for aspergillus and amphotericin B was initiated. On day 32, she had a GI bleed likely related to GVHD and blood counts were decreasing. Schistocytes were noted on day 36. On day 40, she had CMV reactivation and ganciclovir was added. Ruxolitinib was also added for GVHD. At this time her creatinine was increased, thought to be secondary to medications. She continued to have multiple episodes of bacteremia for which gentamicin was added. For her GVHD she also received photopheresis and infliximab. On day 69, she was transferred to the intensive care unit (ICU) for increased abdominal pain and GI bleeding. Echocardiography showed pulmonary arterial hypertension. On day 92 liver GVHD was suspected. She developed Klebsiella pneumonia on day 95. She had a colonoscopy on day 113 which showed mild GVHD and HSCT-TMA.
L2242: Her diagnosis of HSCT-TMA was based on colonoscopy results, in addition to thrombocytopenia, anemia, schistocytes, increased creatinine and elevated LDH, Table 116 shows the patients screening labs. Tacrolimus was discontinued on day 121 and the patient started narsoplimab-wuug that same day. The patient received 8 weekly doses of narsoplimab-wuug, and initial response based on TMA markers occurred approximately 11 days after starting therapy. Response was based on LDH and renal response, as the patient never engrafted platelets and remained blood transfusion dependent.
L2243: Shortly after starting narsoplimab-wuug, the patient developed fever, she was found to have CMV reactivation, in addition to positive blood cultures for fungal and subsequently bacterial infections. She was treated with various antibiotics and antifungals. On day 166 her kidney injury improved. Unfortunately, the patient continued to have a complicated course including viral infections, possible pneumonia, GI-GVHD, transfusion dependence, renal dysfunction, catheter related thrombosis and sepsis. The investigator noted on day 220 there were no signs of HSCT-TMA. The patient ultimately died on day 291 due to GVHD, respiratory failure and sepsis.
L2244: Clinical reviewer comment:
L2245: This patient had very complicated transplant course, most notable for steroid refractory GVHD and recurrent infections for which she was treated with a variety of medications. Tacrolimus was discontinued the same day of narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The treatment of these underlying risk factors may impact the interpretability of narsoplimab-wuug. While not mentioned in the patient narrative, the Applicant was able to confirm the patient did receive a plasma exchange while receiving narsoplimab-wuug therapy. Plasma exchange is not standard of care for HSCT-TMA and response rates have been reported to be 27-80%, but in select patients it may be of benefit (Rosenthal 2016; Jodele 2018; Khosla et al. 2018). Of note, the patient did not have an ADAMTS13 reported.
L2246: The confounders described above make the interpretation of narsoplimab-wuug treatment effect difficult. In addition, the clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as she did not meet all three components of the predefined response definition. While the reviewer understands the patient never engrafted platelet this is a critical component of the response definition and therefore response cannot be substantially determined. Of note, the renal function acutely worsened on therapy, but then significantly down trended as shown in Figure 37. LDH improved on therapy. As for other TMA markers, blood transfusions were continued but this could have been due to poor graft function. Haptoglobin was never decreased.

L2256: Patient (b) (6)
L2257: The patient was a 34-year-old male with a past medical history significant for ALL that was refractory to treatment. He received a matched sibling donor transplant. The patient had a complicated post-transplant course. On day 3, he developed a fungal rash for which micafungin was started and on day 6 he developed neutropenic sepsis (blood culture was positive for coagulase negative Staphylococcus). Platelet engraftment was noted on day 9 and neutrophil engraftment occurred on day 12. The patient had persistent fevers for which meropenem was added. Engraftment syndrome was diagnosed on day 13 and steroids were started. Cefepime and vancomycin were replaced with linezolid. Upper and lower endoscopy showed esophageal
L2258: candidiasis, GVHD, and CMV colitis. On day 30 he developed acute kidney injury suspected to be from dehydration. He was treated with a variety of antibiotics for fevers.
L2259: On day 35 he had thrombocytopenia, anemia and elevated LDH concerning for microangiopathic hemolytic anemia. Cyclosporin was discontinued on day 40 and tacrolimus was started. The patient was screened for Study TMA-001 on day 49 but had a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT) therefore, he was not eligible. He was treated with a reduction in tacrolimus dose and intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG). The patient was re-admitted on day 71 for weakness, elevated creatinine, and mild jaundice. The patient received plasma exchange 12 times without resolution of the TMA and additional IVIG.
L2260: On day 91 he was screened again for enrollment and the DAT was negative. His diagnosis of HSCT-TMA was based on thrombocytopenia, anemia, LDH, and schistocytes, his screening labs are shown in Table 117. On day 107 he had acute kidney injury due to poor oral intake. On day 117 he received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug. He received 8 doses total. Response based on LDH and platelets occurred 228 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. There were no clinical components of the response definition for which he was evaluable.
L2261: The remainder of his post-transplant course was significant for herpes ophthalmicus, chronic GVHD of the skin, and pneumonia.
L2262: Clinical reviewer comment:
L2263: This patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 35 following transplant but, was unable to enroll in Study TMA-001 due to a persistently positive DAT (a key exclusion criteria). The patient received IVIG and plasma exchange but the TMA did not improve. He was ultimately started therapy approximately 80 days after diagnosis. It is unusually to have a positive DAT and HSCT-TMA as this is not immune related process. A positive DAT could indicate an autoimmune hemolytic anemia or Evan's syndrome (Dvorak 2019). Although, at the time of rescreening the investigator did note the patient had schistocytes, which does suggest a microangiopathic hemolytic anemia process. In addition, DAT can become positive with recurrent blood transfusions. The positive DAT certainly complicates the patient's diagnosis.
L2264: The clinical reviewer does not consider the patient to be a responder as he did not meet all three components of the predefined response definition. His response was based on platelets and LDH alone. The patient was never evaluated for clinical improvement as these components were not evaluable, in addition he had a normal creatinine. The Applicant's rationale for including him as a responder was due to an increase in hemoglobin, but this was not a component of the primary endpoint and it's not clear if the magnitude of hemoglobin response is significant with a baseline hemoglobin around 10 g/dL. The other concern was response based on TMA markers (LDH and platelets) was not documented until 228 after starting narsoplimab-wuug. This was discussed with clinical pharmacology colleagues who agreed that based on the half-life of narsoplimab-wuug it's too difficult to determine if this response was related to narsoplimab-wuug. The Applicant states the delay of response was secondary to an incurrent infection but, most patients in Study TMA-001 had infection without a delayed response. Of note, the patient did not meet the inclusion criteria of doubling of creatinine.

L2273: Patient (b) (6)
L2274: The patient was a 57-year-old male with history of relapsed mantle cell lymphoma who underwent a double cord blood transplant. The patient developed veno-occlusive disease on day
L2275: 4. He achieved neutrophil engraftment on day 17. After having intermittent diarrhea, he was diagnosed with GI-GVHD based on sigmoidoscopy. He was treated with solomedrol and budesonide, with improvement. He developed CMV reactivation and was treated with ganciclovir initially which was subsequently changed to fosarnet for increasing viral load. On day 58 he had loss of consciousness and possible seizure, weakness and hallucinations. He was diagnosed with skin GVHD on day 103 and was treated with prednisone and basiliximab. On day 124 he was admitted for weakness and altered mental status. His skin GVHD was ongoing.
L2276: On day 126 he was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA based on thrombocytopenia, decreased haptoglobin, and elevated LDH. Tacrolimus was discontinued. He was also diagnosed with Guillain Barré syndrome (GBS) and was treated with IVIG. CMV reactivation also occurred on that day. Platelet and red blood cell transfusions were started. On day 134 Evan's syndrome was suspected and the patient was treated with rituximab and IVIG. Of note a DAT was checked on day 155 and was negative. On day 144 schistocytes and LDH were increased. The patient entered the study on day 160 (see Table 118 for screening labs) and received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 167. He received 4 total doses. TMA markers improved approximately 35 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug, and transfusions were discontinued.
L2277: The remainder of his transplant course was notable for liver GVHD for which he was treated with steroids and GBS for which he received IVIG. He also developed a positive DAT and was treated with IVIG and rituximab. On day 295 he was started on eltrombopag for pancytopenia.
L2278: Clinical reviewer comment
L2279: Around the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis the patient also had a diagnosis of acute and chronic GVHD, Evan's syndrome, CMV reactivation, and GBS. The patient was actively treated for Evan's syndrome which has overlapping characteristics of HSCT-TMA, such as thrombocytopenia, anemia, and elevated LDH. The clinical reviewer is not convinced that the patient had a diagnosis of Evan's syndrome given the DAT was negative and schistocytes were presents, this is consistent with microangiopathic hemolysis. The patient did receive rituximab for Evan's syndrome shortly before narsoplimab-wuug, there have been a few small studies and case reports suggesting that rituximab may be an effective treatment for HSCT-TMA (Kim et al. 2015). With that being said, timing of response does favor narsoplimab-wuug. Overall, the concurrent disease diagnosis and treatments obscure the treatment effect of narsoplimab-wuug, although favors that this patient responded to narsoplimab-wuug.
L2280: The patient was noted to have a renal response (this was not required to meet the responder definition), but the patient did not meet the renal inclusion criteria of doubling of serum creatinine. With a baseline creatinine of 0.94 mg/dL (screening creatinine was 0.61 mg/dL) and without doubling of the pretransplant creatinine, the significance of the magnitude of reduction is unclear.

He also had CMV reactivation. Around day 178, he was diagnosed with acute skin and liver GVHD, in addition to chronic GVHD of the eye.
L2292: On day 194 he was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA, based on thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH, elevated creatinine and anemia. The patient had been restarted on cyclosporin two weeks prior but this was discontinued once HSCT-TMA was diagnosed. At this time, he was also diagnosed with PRES. With blood pressure control the patient recovered. He also had a low-grade fever. He received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 201, screening labs are shown in Table 119. He received 8 total doses. Response occurred 153 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug and was based on improvement in LDH, platelets, neurologic dysfunction and transfusions. See TMA marker changes in Figure 40. Following initiation of narsoplimab-wuug he had recurrent episodes of delirium and confusion, this was thought to be secondary to steroids, and/or self-discontinuation of lorazepam and haloperidol. He was last seen on day 297 in outpatient clinic.
L2293: Clinical reviewer comment
L2294: The patient was diagnosed and treated for CMV reactivation, fever and GVHD around the same time as his HSCT-TMA diagnosis and starting narsoplimab-wuug. In addition, the patient was diagnosed with PRES at the same time as HSCT-TMA. Neurologic deficits have been reported in up to half of patients with HSCT-TMA and can include confusion, headaches, hallucinations and seizures (Jodele et al. 2015). The most common of CNS injury is likely due to uncontrolled TMA associated hypertension, including PRES (Jodele et al. 2015). While the reviewer acknowledges that PRES can be a manifestation of HSCT-TMA is difficult to tell if improvement was based on hypertension control or narsoplimab-wuug. The timing of PRES improvement is not clear. In addition, the patient had delirium and confusion, but this occurred after the patient had already been started on narsoplimab-wuug. Treatment of concurrent disease diagnosis make it difficult to interpret the treatment effect of narsoplimab-wuug.
L2295: The patient did not meet the responder definition until 153 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. It is difficult to determine if a response this far out from treatment can be attributed to narsoplimab-wuug. The clinical pharmacology team also agreed it is difficult to determine given the half-life of narsoplimab-wuug.
L2296: Of note, the patient also did not meet inclusion/exclusion criteria. The patient had a weakly positive Coombs test (although the repeat central laboratory test was negative) and a positive shiga toxin. Most likely, these tests were false positives given repeat Coombs test was negative and the patient did not have diarrhea.

L2305: Patient (b) (6)
L2306: The patient is a 34-year-old African American male who had a history of S/β0 thalassemia and underwent a matched sibling HSCT, but experienced late graft failure. He then developed myelodysplastic syndrome and underwent a second matched sibling HSCT. On day 10 post-transplant, he had a fever and was treated with antibiotics. On day 14, he had increasing blood pressure and CT of the chest consistent with an atypical infection. Neutrophil engraftment occurred on day 20. On day 23, he developed hematuria and was BK virus positive. He was subsequently diagnosed with CMV reactivation and repeat chest CT showed multifocal infection. He was treated with vancomycin. Platelet engraftment was achieved on day 36. His course was further complicated by head CT showing a subdural hematoma on day 43.
L2307: On day 66 he was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA, supporting his diagnosis was thrombocytopenia, elevated creatinine, increased LDH, anemia, and schistocytes. See screening labs in Table 120. He received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 85. Tacrolimus was discontinued on day 89. He received 4 doses per protocol. His response based on TMA markers occurred 28 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. Response was based on improvement in creatinine, platelets and LDH (see Figure 41).
L2308: Following response to narsoplimab-wuug the patient’s course was complicated by GVHD.
L2309: Clinical reviewer comment
L2310: The patient had tacrolimus discontinued at the time of narsoplimab-wuug treatment. The impact of tacrolimus on this patient’s response appears less likely as the patient did not meet the response definition until 28 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. Therefore, it appears narsoplimab-wuug most likely caused an improvement in TMA markers and serum creatinine.
L2311: The patient’s LDH was just above the upper limit of normal at the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis at 221 U/L (normal range: 100-200U/L). The highest the LDH increased was to 278 U/L and the baseline LDH level was 237 U/L. Therefore, while the LDH may have decreased further on narsoplimab-wuug, it is difficult to determine the significance of magnitude of change. To meet the LDH component of response the patient had to have and LDH &lt;1.5x upper limit of normal (ULN), but this patient never had an LDH &gt;1.5x ULN. The clinical reviewer remains convinced that this patient responded to narsoplimab-wuug given improvement in platelet count and serum creatinine. This is further supported by an increase in hemoglobin (while not receiving transfusions) and improvement in haptoglobin.

L2321: Patient (b) (6)
L2322: The patient was a 46-year-old Caucasian male with a history of relapsed T-ALL, who underwent an unrelated 9/10 matched HSCT. The patient engrafted neutrophils and platelets on day 21. He did well following transplant until day 121 when he developed steroid refractory GI-GVHD. He was treated with pentostatin and mesenchymal stem cells with resolution. He experienced CMV reactivation on day 144 and campylobacter diarrhea on day 186. On day 210, he had a GI biopsy for GI bleeding which demonstrated both GI-GVHD and HSCT-TMA. On day 228, he developed polyneuropathy restricting him to bed which was confirmed on electromyelogram. On day 236, HHV6 infection was identified.
L2323: On day 242 HSCT-TMA was diagnosed based on colonic biopsy, platelet reduction and elevated LDH. The patient also became transfusion dependent. Table 121 shows laboratory markers at time of study entry. The patient received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 263. The patient received 8 weekly doses. Response was based on improvement in LDH, platelets, neurologic dysfunction (polyneuropathy improved on day 290) and GI dysfunction (melena resolved on day 275). Response based on TMA markers occurred 43 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug. See TMA marker changes in Figure 42. Cyclosporin dose was decreased during narsoplimab-wuug treatment.
L2324: Following completion of the clinical trial the patient developed GI and skin GVHD on day 442. He developed Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) reactivation, GI candida infection, and E. coli sepsis that led to his death on day 515.
L2325: Clinical reviewer comment
L2326: While the patient should still be considered a responder, the issues outlined below make the interpretation of the treatment effect of narsoplimab-wuug difficult.
L2327: The patient was determined by the Applicant to have a GI response. The improvement in melena does seem to correlate temporarily to narsoplimab-wuug administration, but this is confounded by the fact the patient also had GI-GVHD and was being treated with steroids during that time. It does not appear that the steroid treatment could account for the GI response alone as the dose had not significantly changed and the patient was on a low dose of prednisone (25 mg). Therefore, it is likely narsoplimab-wuug was responsible for GI improvement.
L2328: The patient was also determined to have a neurologic response based on improvement in polyneuropathy per the Applicant. Polyneuropathy during transplant can be multifactorial including medications. It is difficult to confidently determine if the patient's polyneuropathy is related to HSCT-TMA and if improvement was due to narsoplimab-wuug.
L2329: The patient's response was also confounded by a decreasing cyclosporin dose during narsoplimab-wuug treatment. Of note, the cyclosporin was already at a low dose when it was reduced.
L2330: Based on Figure 42, the patient did have a significant reduction in LDH, and improvement in platelets and haptoglobin.
L2331: Of note, the patient did not meet renal inclusion criteria for study entry, although the renal component of response was not needed to meet the response definition.

The patient developed varicella zoster virus reactivation and HSCT-TMA on day 167 post-transplant, diagnosis was supported by thrombocytopenia, elevated LDH, schistocytes, negative Coomb's test, proteinuria, and anemia. (b) (6) was not receiving
L2361: a CNI inhibitor at the time of (b) (6) HSCT-TMA diagnosis. Around the time of HSCT-TMA diagnosis the patient had infections including sepsis, chronic kidney disease requiring dialysis, seizures, and DAH. The patient failed plasma exchange, defibrotide, and eculizumab. On day 400 post-transplant the patient initiated narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV three times weekly. Following treatment (b) (6) TMA markers improved and (b) (6) was able to discontinue dialysis, respiratory support and transfusions. The patient's HSCT-TMA recurred during narsoplimab-wuug treatment taper in the setting of a respiratory syncytial virus infection. (b) (6) improved with increasing narsoplimab-wuug treatment to the initial frequency. (b) (6) received her last narsoplimab-wuug dose on Day 896 and was still alive on Day 1495.

The cyclosporin dose was tapered but continued through his transplant course. He received his first dose of narsoplimab-wuug on day 62 and received 16 twice weekly 4 mg/kg doses. L2364: Clinical reviewer comment
L2365: In total, the Applicant states 3 out of 5 compassionate use patients responded to narsoplimab-wuug therapy. Compassionate use patients did not use the same response criteria, although based on the narrative descriptions, narsoplimab-wuug did seem to have a positive clinical impact. It is also important to note that compassionate use patients did not receive the same dosing frequency as patients in Study TMA-001. In fact, all patients received more frequent dosing than once weekly. Data collected from the compassionate use patients is limited and the sample size is small (n=5) therefore it is difficult to make conclusions based on the compassionate use program. The compassionate use program will not be included in the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) as efficacy will be determined from Study TMA-001.

L2385: Narratives of Patients Who Died of Treatment-Emergent Deaths During the AE Evaluation Period
L2386: Patient (b) (6) died of neutropenic sepsis 14 days after the last dose of narsoplimab-wuug. The patient was a 58-year-old male with a complicated past medical history including AML, type 2 diabetes mellitus, coronary artery disease, hypertension, hepatitis B and peripheral neuropathy. On (b) (6) the patient received an allogeneic HSCT from a matched related donor for his diagnosis of AML. He received nonmyeloablative conditioning with fludarabine and melphalan. His immunosuppression consisted of tacrolimus and sirolimus. Transplant course was complicated including mucositis, possible typhilitis, diffuse alveolar hemorrhage, possible fungal pneumonia, CMV reactivation, and fluid overload requiring dialysis. He was subsequently diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 21 post-transplant. On (b) (6) he was started on weekly narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV and received 8 weekly doses. On (b) (6) the patient developed
L2387: diarrhea, nausea, and abdominal cramps. He was hospitalized and found to be pancytopenic. Shortly thereafter, he developed bloody diarrhea requiring platelet transfusions and antibiotics. A colonoscopy on (b) (6) revealed diffuse inflammation and erythema. Treatment for GI-GVHD was initiated with tacrolimus, sirolimus, CellCept, Solu-medrol, basiliximab, and later with ruxolitinib. Tacrolimus was discontinued due to encephalopathy. The patient also developed HHV6 reactivation and coagulase-negative staphylococcal bacteremia. By (b) (6) the patient was diagnosed with graft failure versus leukemia reoccurrence. During this time, he developed respiratory distress and hypotension, and required vasopressor support and mechanical ventilation. On (b) (6) the patient died. Sepsis was specified as the most likely cause of death.
L2388: • Patient (b) (6) died of septic shock 3 days following the last dose of narsoplimab-wuug. The patient was a 24-year-old male with a history of relapsed Hodgkin's lymphoma and autologous transplant with subsequent progression of disease. When he was "close to remission" he underwent a matched unrelated donor allogeneic HSCT on (b) (6). He received myeloablative conditioning with fludarabine, total body irradiation, and thymoglobulin. His immunosuppression prophylaxis consisted of cyclosporin and methotrexate. Transplant course was complicated by mucositis, nephrotoxicity, CMV reactivation, renal failure, GI infections, bilateral pneumonia, elevated blood pressure and septic shock. On day 279 post-transplant (b) (6) HSCT-TMA was suspected. On (b) (6) the patient started narsoplimab-wuug 370 mg IV once a week. On (b) (6) he was diagnosed with bilateral pneumonia and was treated with antibiotics, antifungal and antiviral therapies. His condition worsened over the next two days and he was subsequently intubated for respiratory failure. He improved and was extubated, with a resolution of the event on (b) (6). On (b) (6), the patient developed septic shock, blood and urine cultures were positive for Pseudomonas aeruginosa. Despite antibiotics and vasopressors he developed multi-organ dysfunction and died shortly after.• Patient (b) (6) died of graft-versus-host-disease (GVHD) and HSCT-TMA 20 days following the last narsoplimab-wuug dose. The patient was a 61-year-old male with a history of ALL. He underwent a mismatched unrelated donor allogeneic HSCT on (b) (6). He received myeloablative conditioning with busulfan and fludarabine. Immunosuppression consisted of cyclosporin. His transplant course was complicated by E. coli sepsis, mucositis, fever, cytomegalovirus (CMV) reactivation, Coombs-positive autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA), EBV reactivation, fungal pneumonia, GVHD, influenza and respiratory syncytial virus pneumonia. He was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 124 (b) (6). On (b) (6) he started narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV once a week. On (b) (6) he developed fever and diarrhea. Colonoscopy on (b) (6) was diagnostic for GI-GVHD. The GVHD was resistant to steroids, pentostatin, and mesenchymal cells. The patient also developed pneumonia and influenza during this time. Seven days following his last narsoplimab-wuug dose, HSCT-TMA was noted to worsen. On (b) (6) the patient died of multiorgan failure secondary to pneumonia, viral and fungal infections, GI-GVHD and TMA.• Patient (b) (6) died of progressive AML 6 days following the last narsoplimab-wuug dose. The patient was a 48-year-old male with a history of AML in his second complete remission. He underwent a matched related donor allogeneic transplant on (b) (6) and received reduced intensity conditioning. Immunosuppression consisted of cyclosporin and mycophenolate mofetil. His transplant course was complicated by septic shock, sinusitis, choledocholithiasis requiring endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography, GI hemorrhage, and nephrolithiasis. He was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA 31 days following transplant (b) (6). Starting (b) (6) he received 2
L2389: doses of narsoplimab-wuug at 4 mg/kg IV once weekly. The patient's HSCT failed, and he died on (b) (6) The investigator determined this was secondary to his underlying leukemia.
L2390: Clinical reviewer comment
L2391: There were 4 patient deaths in the adverse event (AE) evaluation period. Of those deaths, 2 were related to infections and considered possibly related to narsoplimab-wuug. The other two deaths during the AE evaluation period were unlikely related to narsoplimab-wuug, and more likely related to the underlying disease process such as HSCT-TMA, GVHD, or leukemia. Ultimately, more than half of patients treated on TMA-001 died. This is not unexpected given the patient population consisted of complex, critically ill patients on immunosuppressive therapies, and concurrent disease processes thus increasing their risk for death. In addition, HSCT-TMA has a high mortality rate.
L2392: Supportive Safety Population
L2393: In patients with aHUS and TTP, 3 of 30 patients (10%) in Study TMA-001 and 1 of 6 patients (16.7%) in Study OMS-HUS-002 died during the study (Table 126). The most common cause were infections, including Escherichia sepsis and pneumonia. There were two deaths in compassionate use patients. Narratives are described below.
L2394: There were no deaths in the NHV studies, OMS721-GNP-001 or OMS721-IGA-001.
L2395: Patient Narratives
L2396: • Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 54-year-old female in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, asthma, heart failure, and arterial hypertension who died due to the treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) of pneumonia. The patient had received 7 doses of 4 mg/kg IV narsoplimab-wuug when she developed grade 4 pneumonia supported by a bronchoscopy which demonstrated suppurative pneumonia. Respiratory cultures were positive for Burkholderia cepacia and she was treated with antibiotics. During this time narsoplimab-wuug was continued. Shortly after the patient developed respiratory failure with cardiac arrest and died. The patient's death occurred 64 days from the first dose of narsoplimab-wuug.• Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 43-year-old female in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, hypertension, and gastritis, who died of a TEAE of sepsis. The patient received 5 scheduled doses of narsoplimab-wuug, in addition to supplemental doses while receiving plasma exchange therapy. During hospitalization the patient hallucinated and experienced convulsions. A CT scan was negative, and the patient was started on valproic acid and haloperidol. Following the initiation of valproic acid the patient developed pancytopenia, but this resolved after discontinuation. A little over two weeks after recovery from the pancytopenia the patient developed an infection, blood cultures grew Escherichia coli and Klebsiella pneumonia. Despite treatment with IV antibiotics and immunoglobins the patient deteriorated and died.
L2397: • Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 73-year-old male in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, chronic parenchymal liver disease, and hyperammonemia, who died of a TEAE of sudden death. After 5 doses of narsoplimab-wuug 4 mg/kg IV the patient was admitted to the hospital for hematemesis. An endoscopy showed esophageal varices and portal hypertensive gastropathy. He continued on dialysis for his end-stage renal disease. Approximately 2.5 months later the patient developed massive ascites. The patient recovered from the event. Approximately 10 days after recovery and discharge from the hospital the patient had lost consciousness at home and was pronounced dead at the hospital. At the time of death, the patient had received 19 doses of narsoplimab-wuug. Cause of death was not determined.• Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 40-year-old male in Study OMS721-HUS-002, with aHUS, epilepsy, arterial hypertension, renal failure, type 2 diabetes mellitus, mediastinal abscesses, and pancreatitis, who died due to TEAEs of Escherichia coli and intracerebral hematoma. Prior to starting narsoplimab-wuug the patient was hospitalized for seizures. A CT scan showed a new left subdural hematoma and bilateral hygroma. Approximately a week later a repeat CT showed bilateral chronic subdural hematomas with active bleeding with mass effect. The patient had neurologic deterioration. Shortly after this event narsoplimab-wuug was initiated. Approximately 14 days after starting narsoplimab-wuug the patient became unresponsive. Head CT showed numerous hypodense foci in the white matter of both hemispheres, followed by a repeat CT which showed evolution of the subdural hematomas. In addition, the patient developed GI bleeding and blood culture was positive for E. coli. Despite antibiotic therapy the patient died of sepsis and intracerebral hematoma. It was also noted the patient had grade 4 thrombocytopenia which likely contributed to the bleeding events.• Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 48-year-old male with HSCT-TMA treated on compassionate use. The patient had a complicated medical history consisting of HIV-1, latent tuberculosis, Hodgkin's lymphoma with nodular sclerosis, renal failure, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease and cardiotoxicity. He received an unrelated mismatched donor allogeneic transplant. The conditioning regimen included, cyclophosphamide, fludarabine, busulfan, and thymoglobulin, and cell source was umbilical cord. Tacrolimus was started prior to transplant, the patient developed HSCT-TMA on day 0 (same day as HSCT). He started narsoplimab-wuug on day 6. Post-transplant course was complicated by cocaine and heroin abstinence syndrome, human papillomavirus, E. faecalis bacteremia, and CMV viremia on day 15. Shortly after he developed febrile neutropenia and severe esophagitis. Bronchoalveolar lavage was positive for aspergillus. He died of cardiac arrest on day 31. He received 8 twice weekly narsoplimab-wuug doses of 4 mg/kg from day 6 to day 31.• Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 31-year-old female with HSCT-TMA treated on compassionate use. The patient had a history of severe aplastic anemia. She received a matched unrelated donor allogeneic transplant. She received reduced intensity conditioning with fludarabine, cyclophosphamide, antithymocyte globulin, and total body irradiation. She was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on day 11. She received 3 doses of narsoplimab-wuug on day 20 to 27 and plasma infusions. She required blood and platelet transfusions, in addition to hemodialysis. She experienced a left temporal hemorrhagic cerebral infarction on day 28 and died on day 31. Cause of death was listed as HSCT-TMA, acute GVHD, and postallogeneic stem cell transplant aplastic anemia.

L2410: Patient (b) (6): Patient is a 20 year old male, healthy volunteer, on study OMS721-NHV-002 who discontinued narsoplimab-wuug for a TEAE of supraventricular tachycardia. On Day 1, he received narsoplimab-wuug 6.0 mg/kg subcutaneously. On Day 8, he received a second
L2411: dose of narsoplimab-wuug 6.0 mg/kg IV after which he reported no TEAEs. No further information was provided.
L2412: • Patient (b) (6): Patient is a 28-year-old woman on study OMS721-HUS-002, with aHUS, poorly controlled hypertension, kidney hematoma, obesity and hemodialysis dependent end stage renal disease. The patient discontinued narsoplimab-wuug due to TEAEs of grade 3 urinary tract infection and grade 4 liver insufficiency. The patient started narsoplimab-wuug with the protocol-defined induction regimen on (b) (6). While receiving narsoplimab-wuug on (b) (6) the patient was admitted to hospital with fever following a seafood meal. The patient's partner had gastroenteritis following the seafood meal. The patient had signs of liver insufficiency, hypotension, and hypoxia. Urinalysis was consistent with a urinary tract infection. Imaging demonstrated an enlarged liver with signs of steatosis and a persistent left kidney hematoma. Viral serologies for hepatitis and cytomegalovirus (CMV) were negative. Herpes simplex virus and EBV were also negative. The patient denied alcohol, acetaminophen, herbal supplements, and over-the-counter products. The etiology of the patient's fever was subsequently determined to be due to a urinary tract infection and she was treated with broad spectrum antibiotics. Shortly thereafter (b) (6) the patient was diagnosed with liver insufficiency which met Hy's Law criteria. Narsoplimab-wuug was discontinued (37 days after the first dose). Laboratory results showed gradual decrease in aminotransferase levels and simultaneous increase in total (275 \mu\text{mol/L}), direct bilirubin (156 \mu\text{mol/L}) and ammonia (239.6 \mu\text{g/L}). Coagulation abnormalities were also observed. Follow-up scans revealed a liver of normal size, granular echogenicity, and a rim of fluid in the area of the gallbladder. The patient had hemodialysis and plasmapheresis for an exacerbation of aHUS. She recovered from her liver insufficiency and was discharged home on (b) (6).• Patient (b) (6): The patient is a 40-year-old male in Study TMA-001, with aHUS, IgA nephropathy, end stage renal disease and renal transplant. The patient had a history of Mycobacterium haemophilum over a three-year period, approximately 5 years prior to receiving narsoplimab-wuug. The patient discontinued narsoplimab-wuug due to a TEAE of granulomatous orchitis. The patient received 0.675 mg/kg IV narsoplimab-wuug, after receiving 3 doses of narsoplimab-wuug he reported right testicular swelling. Testicular biopsy demonstrated granulomatous disease and no malignant cells. Cultures were negative, including for Mycobacterium. The event resolved approximately one month later. Narsoplimab-wuug was discontinued.

L2437: 90-Day Safety Update
L2438: The 90-Day Safety Update presents additional safety data collected from the 2 ongoing clinical trials from December 21, 2019 to August 31, 2020 (Studies OMS721-HUS-002 and OMS721-IGA-001) and safety data from compassionate use patients with HSCT-TMA from December 21, 2019, to October 31, 2020. Deaths, study drug discontinuations and serious adverse events (SAEs) were reviewed. Overall, there was no change in the risk/benefit assessment based on the 90-day safety update. There were four deaths described in the 90-day safety update. This included one sudden death in study OMS721-IgA-001 and three deaths in compassionate use patients. The narratives are described below.
L2439: • Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 55 year old M with IgA nephropathy, with a past medical history of coronary artery disease and acute myocardial infarction, who was on blinded study treatment once weekly IV narsoplimab-wuug per protocol and completed the extended study therapy on (b) (6). On (b) (6) the patient died suddenly due to a pulmonary embolism. No further information was provided.
• Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 72 year old female with HSCT-TMA, treated on compassionate use, who died due to an event of respiratory failure. On (b) (6) (prior to starting narsoplimab-wuug) the patient developed dyspnea and was hospitalized
L2440: with bilateral pleural effusions and leukopenia. The patient was subsequently intubated due to respiratory failure. On (b) (6) the patient improved and was transferred out of the ICU. On (b) (6), she started treatment with narsoplimab-wuug, 370 mg IV twice a week. On (b) (6) the patient developed acute respiratory failure and was transferred to the ICU for BiPAP therapy. She had a bronchoscopy and thoracentesis. Additional findings revealed pneumonia, pulmonary edema, acute respiratory distress syndrome, acute kidney injury, pressure ulcer, septic shock and empyema of the chest. The patient received her last dose of narsoplimab-wuug on (b) (6) with no reported dose interruptions. The patient died on (b) (6) due to respiratory failure.
L2441: • Patient (b) (6): The patient was a 16-year-old male, in the compassionate use program, with lymphoproliferative disease and HSCT-TMA, who discontinued treatment after receiving 3 doses of 370 mg IV narsoplimab-wuug due to deterioration. The patient was diagnosed with HSCT-TMA on (b) (6). Treatment was initiated with eculizumab and therapeutic plasma exchange. Eculizumab was discontinued, but subsequently restarted due to a relapse of HSCT-TMA on (b) (6). His course was complicated by further progression of TMA markers, renal replacement therapy and worsening hypertension. The patient developed PRES and septic shock which resolved. On (b) (6), the patient discontinued eculizumab and a few weeks later began narsoplimab-wuug per the compassionate use program at 370 mg IV, 2 times per week. After receiving three doses the patient had a general deterioration and discontinued narsoplimab-wuug. The patient was discharged home with hospice care and died at home. Cause of death was reported as renal failure.• Patient (b) (6): The patient was a (b) (6) female, in the compassionate use program, with Diamond Blackfan Anemia had a bone marrow transplant was suspected to have HSCT-TMA 14 days following transplant. She was started on eculizumab. On day 79 (date not included) she continued to have HSCT-TMA and a pericardial effusion. On (b) (6), narsoplimab-wuug was started. She received two doses before the drug was interrupted for concern of sepsis. She switched to plasmapheresis, and decompensated requiring mechanical ventilation with cardiac support. The patient died on (b) (6).
